4
Introduction
This report examines the mathematics results for New Zealand Year 5 students from 
the Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) in 2014/151. 
Along with the reports on New Zealand’s results for mathematics at Year 9, and for 
science at Years 5 and 9, this report forms part of a series of publications about 
New Zealand’s participation in TIMSS 2014/15 (Caygill, Hanlar, & Singh, 2016a,b; 
Caygill, Singh & Hanlar, 2016). International findings for mathematics for TIMSS 
2014/15 have been published by the IEA2 along with a separate international report 
for science (Mullis, Martin, Foy, & Hooper, 2016; Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016). 
This report examines New Zealand’s mathematics achievement in relation to other 
countries that participated in the study, as well as trends at the Year 5 level from 
1994 to 2014. An examination of the TIMSS assessment questions in relation to 
New Zealand’s mathematics curriculum is presented, followed by analyses of 
achievement by sub-groupings (such as gender and ethnicity) and student 
background factors. Comprehensive coverage of background questions about 
teaching and learning, as well as the school context for learning, is also provided.
1 	
Internationally this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 2015. As New Zealand conducted TIMSS towards the end of 2014, it is 
referred to as TIMSS 2014/15 throughout this report.
2	
 The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) is an independent, international cooperative 
of national research institutions and governmental research agencies. It conducts large-scale comparative studies of educational 
achievement and other aspects of education.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

What is TIMSS?
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is a large-scale comparative study of 
mathematics and science achievement at the fourth and eighth grades (Years 5 and 9) around the world. 
As well as examining student achievement, it also monitors curricular implementation and aims to identify 
the most promising instructional practices from around the world.
Conducted on a regular four-year cycle, TIMSS has assessed mathematics and science in 1994/953, 1998/99, 
2002/03, 2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15, with planning underway internationally for 2018/19.
A consortium is responsible for managing the international activities required for the project. This consortium 
consists of: the International Study Center, Lynch School of Education at Boston College, (Massachusetts) 
United States; the IEA Secretariat in Amsterdam, the Netherlands; the IEA’s Data Processing Centre in 
Hamburg, Germany; Statistics Canada in Ottawa, Canada; and the Educational Testing Service (ETS) in 
Princeton, New Jersey in the United States. In New Zealand the Comparative Education Research Unit in the 
Ministry of Education is responsible for carrying out TIMSS.
What value does TIMSS offer?
Although TIMSS is useful for international benchmarking purposes, the real value of TIMSS lies in its ability to 
provide a rich picture of mathematics and science achievement within New Zealand over time.
TIMSS (along with other international assessment studies) can provide information about the performance 
of the New Zealand education system at the national level within a global context. The information from 
studies such as TIMSS is used in the development and review of policy frameworks, and also to inform and 
improve teaching practice. Developments arising out of previous cycles of TIMSS include resource materials 
for schools and teachers, along with teacher in-service training programmes.
What does TIMSS consist of?
TIMSS consists of assessments of students’ achievements in mathematics and science, along with 
questionnaires for students, their parents, teachers, and principals to gather background information. 
The background information provides a context within which the achievement can be examined.
The TIMSS assessments were developed cooperatively with representatives from participating countries. 
Questions were field-tested with a representative sample of students in participating countries, and 
the results generated were used to select and refine the questions for the final test. Questions for the 
background questionnaires underwent a similar process.
Assessment framework for mathematics in TIMSS
As part of the TIMSS methodology, the international consortium develops an assessment framework which 
guides the development of the test items and the context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The 
TIMSS assessments are organised around two dimensions: a content dimension specifying the domains or 
subject matter to be assessed within mathematics and science; and a cognitive dimension specifying the 
domains or thinking processes to be assessed. 
These domains differ for middle primary and lower secondary students to reflect the mathematics curriculum 
taught at each level. The three content dimensions for mathematics at the middle primary level (Year 5 level 
in New Zealand) are number, geometric shapes and measures, and data display. 
3	
 Note that this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 1995 internationally as most countries participated in 1995. However, southern 
hemisphere countries conducted the assessment towards the end of 1994 so in New Zealand reports the study is referred to as 
TIMSS 1994/95. Similarly for the subsequent cycles, the two years in which administrations occurred in participating countries 
are indicated.

In order to answer questions in the TIMSS test correctly, as well as being familiar with the mathematics 
content, students need to draw on a range of cognitive skills. This is acknowledged in the framework under 
the cognitive domain as:
»»
knowing – covers the facts, concepts, and procedures students need to know;
»»
applying – focuses on the ability of students to apply knowledge and conceptual understanding to solve 
problems or answer questions;
»»
reasoning – goes beyond the solution of routine problems to encompass unfamiliar situations, complex 
contexts, and multi-step problems. 
Exhibit 1 below shows how the TIMSS framework matches to the New Zealand Curriculum and the content 
and cognitive demands that are expected at the middle primary level (Grade 4 or Year 5). Refer to Chapter 3 
for more information on the framework, especially around the content and cognitive domains.

Who participated?
In TIMSS 2014/15, approximately 312,000 students, 250,000 parents, 20,000 teachers, and 10,000 
schools from 49 countries (two of which tested students at a higher grade) and 7 economies (known as 
benchmarking participants) took part at the middle primary level4. A list of countries and benchmarking 
participants at the middle primary level can be found in Exhibit 2. Benchmarking participants will not be 
referred to in this report.
In this cycle of TIMSS, both Year 5 and Year 9 students from New Zealand participated. Schools in New 
Zealand were sampled so that there was no overlap between the samples. In New Zealand, there were 6,321 
students, 460 teachers and 3,424 parents from 167 schools assessed at the Year 5 level in October and 
November 2014.

4	
Some countries only tested students who were much older than the target population. For example, middle primary students 
should be around 10 years old according to the design of TIMSS (in the fourth grade or the year level where the average age is 
closest to 10). However, in some countries these children have not covered enough of the material to achieve adequately on the 
TIMSS tests so the country has decided to test much older children. Throughout this report the countries that tested at a higher 
grade and the benchmarking participants are not discussed and do not appear in any totals or comparisons.

How was TIMSS administered?
Each middle primary student was assessed in two timed sessions of 36 minutes, and answered a combination 
of mathematics and science questions. The assessment was a pencil-and-paper test containing both 
multiple-choice and constructed-response questions. Following this, students were given a questionnaire 
containing questions about themselves, their opinions about mathematics and science, their computer use, 
their attitudes to school and leisure activities. Principals, teachers, and parents were also given questionnaires 
in order to gain further information about the context in which mathematics teaching and learning take 
place. In New Zealand, the assessments and questionnaires were conducted in English5.
What procedures were used to ensure the quality of the 
data?
Members of the consortium ensured procedures were adhered to by all participating countries. TIMSS 
procedures are designed to ensure the reliability, validity, and comparability of the data through:
»»
careful planning and documentation;
»»
cooperation among participating countries;
»»
standardised procedures;
»»
attention to quality control throughout. 
Procedures included:
»»
verification of translations and layout of booklets and questionnaires;
»»
monitoring of sampling activities;
»»
international and national quality control observers during test administration;
»»
checking of data;
»»
detailed manuals covering procedures;
»»
rigorous training for all involved.
Further information
A lot of information is gathered during the TIMSS administration, and a number of techniques are applied 
when collecting and analysing the data. The Methods and procedures in TIMSS 2015 report (Martin, Mullis, & 
Hooper (Eds.), 2016) contains a detailed account of the assessment framework and instrument development, 
sampling, translation of materials, survey operations, quality assurance, creating the international databases, 
and scaling the achievement data. In addition, when the database is made available, it will be accompanied 
by a guide on how to conduct analyses. Brief details of the technical information are given in the definitions 
and technical notes at the end of this report.
In order to provide a context in which the TIMSS results can be examined, TIMSS also publishes the TIMSS 
2015 encyclopedia: Education policy and curriculum in mathematics and science (Mullis, Martin, Goh, & Cotter 
(Eds.), 2016). This encyclopaedia contains short reports from each country describing mathematics and 
science education policies and practices in that country.
5	
In 2002/03, tests and questionnaires were also translated into te reo Māori, but in order to make comparisons between each of 
the cycles, these students were excluded from analyses presented in this report.

10
Mathematics achievement 
of New Zealand Year 5 
students since 1994 
– 20 years of trends
CHAPTER 1:
 Key Findings
The average (mean) mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students in 2014/15 
was higher than that of the students 20 years ago, but was not significantly different from 
2002/03, 2006/07, and 2010/11.
The gap between the high and low achievers (range) for Year 5 students is wider in 2014/15 
than in 2010/11, but not as wide as 1994/95.
Although New Zealand’s mean achievement in mathematics has increased since 1994/95, many 
other countries have increased by more.
New Zealand Year 5 students’ mean mathematics achievement was significantly higher than 13 
countries, but lower than the mean score of 33 countries, including all the other predominantly 
English-speaking countries who participated.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
11
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
Mathematics is vital in human learning and development as well as knowledge 
creation. The nineteenth century German mathematician, astronomer and physicist 
Carl Friedrich Gauss is believed to even claim it to be the queen of all sciences. The 
importance of mathematics in the development of modern high-tech fields such 
as computer science and digital technologies, engineering, economic analysis and 
forecasting, statistics and many others is self-evident. 
In 2014 New Zealand participated in the sixth cycle of TIMSS, a large-scale 
assessment of the mathematics and science skills and knowledge of middle primary 
and lower secondary students. This sixth cycle of TIMSS occurred 20 years after 
the first TIMSS assessment, so we now have information about their achievement 
over this period. Forty-nine countries, including New Zealand, participated in this 
cycle conducted during 2014 and 2015. This chapter will examine the mathematics 
achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students in relation to that of other participating 
countries, and over time.
Mathematics achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
New Zealand’s mathematics achievement in TIMSS can be examined as a snapshot in time compared to 
other countries, but also over time. This first section begins with New Zealand’s achievement in the 2014/15 
cycle of TIMSS. For reporting, the achievement of all participating students has been put on a scale with a 
centre point of 500. This point of 500 was set using the student performance in the 1994/95 cycle of TIMSS, 
and achievement in subsequent cycles was put on the same scale. Achievement was then reported relative 
to this scale using the term scale score points (see Definitions and technical notes for further details on the 
scales and the centre point).
The mean mathematics score for New Zealand Year 5 students in 2014/15 was 491 scale score points. 
New Zealand’s score was significantly lower than the TIMSS scale centre point, but similar to France (488) 
and Turkey (483), and higher than 13 countries. However, New Zealand’s mean score (491) was lower than 
the mean score of 33 countries, including all the other predominantly English-speaking countries who 
participated. Scotland, a predominantly English-speaking country with a similar score to New Zealand in 
earlier cycles, did not participate in this cycle.
The highest achieving countries, Singapore, Hong Kong SAR, and the Republic of Korea, all had average 
achievement among their middle primary students of over 600 scale score points. Of the countries that 
tested in English, Singapore had the highest mean score (618). The next highest mean scores among the 
countries testing in English were Northern Ireland (570), Ireland (547), and England (546).
It is also useful to look at the range of achievement – the gap between the high and low achievers. 
The lowest outer limit of the bars presented in Exhibit 1.1 is called the 5th percentile, the score at which only 
five percent of students achieved a lower score. The upper-most limit presented is the 95th percentile, the 
score at which only five percent of students achieved a higher score. The range of achievement from the 5th 
percentile (335) to the 95th percentile (632) for New Zealand Year 5 students was 297 scale score points. 
New Zealand’s range of achievement was wider than the high-performing countries, and all the English 
speaking countries. This was a different result from the previous cycle, where New Zealand’s range was fairly 
moderate compared with other countries in TIMSS. This change was mostly due to an increase in the 95th 
percentile, i.e., more students have higher achievement. 
The range from the 5th to the 95th percentile can move around quite a lot from cycle to cycle. Therefore it 
was useful to also look at the inter-quartile range, the difference between the 25th and 75th percentile, for 
a more stable version of the range. The inter-quartile range of New Zealand Year 5 students (121) was still 
higher than most of the high-performing countries.

Exhibit 1.2 provides information to help put mathematics achievement in context. It shows selected structural 
characteristics of education, including expenditure on education, to help the reader understand similarities 
and differences between New Zealand and other participating countries. Countries are presented in the same 
order as Exhibit 1.1 for ease of comparison. The GNI (purchasing power parity) is a measure of income for 
a country converted to U.S. dollars and adjusted to allow comparison of real levels of expenditure between 
countries (see http://databank.worldbank.org for details).
Many of the high-achieving countries had much higher income per capita than New Zealand, especially when 
purchasing power was taken into account. The exception was the Republic of Korea, whose GNI was a bit 
smaller than New Zealand and a lot smaller than the other high achieving countries. In terms of the countries 
that tested in English, all but Malta had higher income per capita than New Zealand.
Trends in New Zealand’s achievement over 6 cycles of TIMSS
Mean mathematics achievement steadily increased between 1994 and 2002 (see Exhibit 1.3). Between 2002 
and 2011, however, the mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students decreased. Achievement 
in 2014 was not statistically different from 2002, 2006, or 2011. However, the 2014 result appears to signal 
a turnaround and does not continue the downward trend observed between 2002 and 2011. The mean 
achievement of Year 5 students in 2014 was significantly higher than in the first cycle in 1994.

As mentioned earlier, it is also useful to look at the gap between the high and low achievers (range). 
Since 1994, there has been concern about the proportion of low-achieving Year 5 students. Therefore, it is 
important to explore whether any changes have happened across the spectrum of achievement. Exhibit 
1.4 presents achievement at the 5th, 25th, 75th, and 95th percentiles. As shown in Exhibit 1.4, the range of 
achievement has widened since 2011, but is not as wide as it was in 1994. The widening has happened at both 
ends of the spectrum; the 5th percentile is lower and the 95th percentile higher than in 2002.

Trends on the mathematics test questions from 2010/11 to 
2014/15
At the end of each cycle of TIMSS, test questions are released into the public domain. At the beginning of 
the next cycle, new questions are developed to replace released questions. In addition, each cycle of TIMSS 
includes some questions from previous cycles to provide a trend measure over time. This section presents an 
analysis of the trend questions included in both TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15.
There were 102 questions common to both 2010/11 and 2014/15. Of these 102 questions, 13 questions had 
similar proportions of New Zealand Year 5 students correctly answering them across the two cycles (as 
shown in Exhibit 1.5). More than three-quarters of the questions (79) showed an increase; that is they were 
correctly answered by more students in 2014/15 compared with 2010/11. In contrast, less than one-tenth 
of the questions showed a decrease; that is they were correctly answered by fewer students in 2014/15 
compared with 2010/11.
Although the overall mean difference of five scale score points between 2010/11 and 2014/15 was not 
significant, these item statistics may be indicative of an upward trend.

International trends in mathematics achievement at the 
middle primary level
There have now been five cycles of TIMSS internationally at the middle primary level, 1994/95, 2002/03, 
2006/07, 2010/11, and 2014/15 (although New Zealand repeated the 1994/95 study in 1998 with our Year 5 
students, this was not an international option). The design of TIMSS allows us to measure trends over time. 
Exhibit 1.6 presents changes in mean mathematics achievement for those countries that have participated 
in selected cycles of TIMSS. England was the country with the largest increase in mathematics achievement 
since the 1994/95 cycle, although most of this change happened prior to the 2002/03 cycle. Slovenia, Hong 
Kong SAR, and the Islamic Republic of Iran have also had large increases in mathematics achievement since 
1994/95. New Zealand, along with the Singapore, Japan, Australia, the United States, and Norway, have had 
more moderate increases over time. However, Norway had a significant decrease between 1994/95 and 
2002/03 that has since been reversed and improved upon.

In addition to those countries presented in the table, Portugal (99 scale score points) and Cyprus (48 scale 
score points) have also had large increases in mathematics achievement since 1994/95. Morocco, though 
starting from a low of 335 scale score points, has had a large increase since 2010/11 (43 scale score points).
Relative rankings among countries
Many commentators on the international studies focus on New Zealand’s ranking relative to other countries. 
In order to inform this commentary, Exhibit 1.7 presents New Zealand’s relative ranking in mathematics 
achievement compared with the other countries who have participated in TIMSS in 1994/95, 2002/03, 
and 2014/15. Of all the 48 countries that participated in TIMSS 2014/15 at the middle primary level, only 13 
have participated in all these three cycles. In addition, standard errors are presented so that the reader can 
calculate whether apparent differences are real. For example, the score of 531 in England (2002/03) is not 
significantly different from the score of 529 in Hungary (see section entitled Definitions and technical notes 
for details of how significance can be calculated).
Exhibit 1.7 shows that the mean mathematics achievement in New Zealand has been below the mean for 
the 13 trend countries in each cycle. In addition, while the mean for New Zealand has increased compared 
with 1994/95, so has the mean for all 13 countries. Therefore, the ranking of New Zealand among these 13 
countries is at its lowest in 2014/15 compared to the previous cycles.

Classifying New Zealand 
Year 5 students as low 
to advanced performers 
CHAPTER 2:
 Key Findings
Six percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were classified as advanced performers (reached 
the advanced benchmark), while 16 percent were classified as below low performers who did 
not perform simple mathematical tasks. 
Compared with other countries, New Zealand was around the middle when considering the 
proportion of advanced performers, but had a high proportion of lower performers. High 
performing countries had more than one-third of their middle primary students classified as 
advanced performers, and one percent or fewer classified as below low performers.
The proportions of Year 5 students reaching each classification in 2014/15 were not significantly 
different from 2002/03 (the TIMSS cycle with the best results). The proportion of high-
achieving students, those classified as high or advanced performers, has increased significantly 
between 2010/11 and 2014/15.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

International mathematics benchmarks
The international benchmarks are four points on the mathematics scale: the advanced benchmark (625 scale 
score points), the high benchmark (550), the intermediate benchmark (475), and the low benchmark (400). 
The performance of students reaching each benchmark is described in relation to the types of questions 
they answered correctly. Exhibit 2.1 presents the 2014/15 descriptions of the international benchmarks of 
mathematics achievement.
Exhibit 2.1: Descriptions of TIMSS 2014/15 international benchmarks of mathematics achievement
Advanced international benchmark — 625
Students can apply their understanding and knowledge in a variety of relatively complex situations and explain their reasoning. They 
can solve a variety of multi-step word problems involving whole numbers. Students at this level show an increasing understanding of 
fractions and decimals. They can apply knowledge of a range of two- and three-dimensional shapes in a variety of situations. They can 
interpret and represent data to solve multi-step problems.
High international benchmark — 550
Students can apply their knowledge and understanding to solve problems. They can solve word problems involving operations with whole 
numbers, simple fractions, and two-place decimals. Students demonstrate understanding of geometric properties of shapes and of angles 
that are less than or greater than a right angle. Students can interpret and use data in tables and a variety of graphs to solve problems.
Intermediate international benchmark — 475
Students can apply basic mathematical knowledge in simple situations. They demonstrate an understanding of whole numbers and 
some understanding of fractions and decimals. Students can relate two- and three-dimensional shapes and identify and draw shapes 
with simple properties. They can read and interpret bar graphs and tables.
Low international benchmark — 400
Students have some basic mathematical knowledge. They can add and subtract whole numbers, have some understanding of 
multiplication by one-digit numbers, and can solve simple word problems. They have some knowledge of simple fractions, geometric 
shapes, and measurement. Students can read and complete simple bar graphs and tables.
Source:	 Exhibit 2.1, Mullis, Martin, Foy, & Hooper, 2016.
In order to describe more fully what achievement on the mathematics scale means, 
the TIMSS international researchers have developed benchmarks. These benchmarks 
link student performance on the TIMSS mathematics scale to performance on 
mathematics questions, and describe what students can typically do at set points 
on the mathematics achievement scale. These benchmarks classify students by 
the ability they demonstrated in the TIMSS assessment. This chapter describes 
New Zealand Year 5 students’ mathematics achievement using these benchmarks 
compared to other countries and over time.

20
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
Exhibit 2.2 presents the benchmarks for all countries in two ways. The graphical part shows the proportions 
of students achieving at each of the benchmarks (i.e. above the high benchmark of 550 but not above 
the advanced benchmark of 625). The table part shows the proportions of students achieving at or above 
each of the benchmarks (i.e. at or above the high benchmark of 550, including those above the advanced 
benchmark). Six percent of New Zealand Year 5 students reached the advanced benchmark, the point where 
students were deemed capable of applying their understanding and knowledge in a variety of relatively 
complex situations and explaining their reasoning. In comparison, half the students in Singapore, and more 
than one-third of the students in the Republic of Korea, Hong Kong SAR, and Chinese Taipei reached this 
advanced level of mathematics ability. There were also fewer advanced middle primary mathematicians in 
New Zealand compared with England (17%), the United States (14%), and Australia (9%).
Sixteen percent of Year 5 students in New Zealand could not perform many of the simplest tasks TIMSS 
seeks to measure (they correctly completed less than half of the low benchmark tasks). In comparison only 
one percent or fewer of students in the highest-performing countries performed below this low benchmark. 
There were also fewer really low performing middle primary students (those who did not reach the low 
benchmark) in England (4%), the United States (5%), and Australia (9%).
Included at the bottom of the exhibit is the international median percentage of students at each benchmark. 
The proportion of New Zealand students reaching the advanced benchmark was the same as the 
international median percentage. However, for the other benchmarks, high, intermediate, and low, fewer 
New Zealand students reached these compared with the international median.

Changes over time for New Zealand Year 5 students on 
mathematics benchmarks
Since TIMSS began, there has been some concern about the proportion of New Zealand students who have 
low achievement in mathematics. We can use the benchmarks to compare how this proportion has changed 
over time. Exhibit 2.3 presents those Year 5 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown in the 
graphical part) and those students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in the table 
part) in each cycle from 1994 to 2014.
As noted earlier, the range of mathematics achievement has widened since 2002, with the 5th percentile 
getting lower and the 95th percentile getting higher. However, the proportion of students reaching each 
benchmark in 2014 was not significantly different from 2002. The proportion of high-achieving students, 
those reaching the high and advanced benchmarks, has increased significantly between 2011 and 2014. 
Even though there was not a significant change between 2002 and 2014, the proportion of students not 
reaching the low benchmark (16%) remains a concern. These students below the low benchmark did not 
demonstrate basic mathematical knowledge. Some of the national standards at the Year 4 level appear more 
complex than the low benchmark (for example interpreting statistical displays; using the statistical enquiry 
cycle independently; create, continue, and give the rule for sequential patterns with two variables).

Strengths and weaknesses 
of New Zealand Year 
5 students within 
mathematics
CHAPTER 3:
 Key Findings
New Zealand Year 5 students consistently, since 2006/07, show greatest strength at statistics 
questions (called data display in TIMSS) compared to number and geometric shapes and 
measures. 
New Zealand Year 5 students do significantly better at applying their knowledge and 
reasoning, compared to the cognitive behaviour of knowing. This has been a consistent pattern 
since 2006/07.

24
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
To measure trends across time, some of the items from each cycle are kept secure 
and used in the next cycle. The assessment in 2014/15 had items from the previous 
two cycles, as well as new items. In total there were 169 test questions at the 
middle primary level in mathematics (Year 5 in New Zealand). 
As outlined in the introductory chapter, the international consortium developed the 
TIMSS 2014/15 assessment framework, which guided the development of the test 
items and the context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The assessment 
framework was matched to the curriculum of participating countries to reflect the 
curricular emphasis across countries, and is further explored in Chapter 4. This 
chapter focuses on the strengths and weaknesses of New Zealand Year 5 students 
in mathematics across the two dimensions that make up the TIMSS 2014/15 
assessment framework, the content and cognitive domains.
Defining the mathematics content and cognitive domains
The TIMSS middle primary assessment is designed to cover a wide range of the mathematics teaching and 
learning students around the world are likely to encounter. Like the New Zealand Curriculum, the assessment 
framework is arranged by areas of mathematics, and details the content the assessment will cover, termed 
the content domains. In addition, the thinking processes and behaviours expected as students engage with 
the content range from simple knowledge recall to higher-order thinking skills, termed the cognitive domains 
(Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The content domains for mathematics at the middle primary level are number, 
geometric shapes and measures, and data display. The cognitive domains are entitled knowing, applying, and 
reasoning.
At the middle primary level, the number domain focuses on students’ understandings and skills related to 
whole numbers, fractions and decimals, expressions, simple equations and relationships between numbers. 
The geometric shapes and measures domain focuses on students’ abilities to identify characteristics and 
properties of geometrical figures, which include two- and three-dimensional shapes. Students should also be 
able to measure attributes such as lengths, areas, angles and volumes. The data display domain focuses on 
students’ ability to read and represent data.
The TIMSS cognitive domain knowing encompasses being able to recall facts, conventions, and concepts, 
along with performing skills such as measuring, classifying, ordering, and computing. Applying mathematics 
knowledge in a range of contexts is the core of the applying domain. The reasoning domain includes 
analysing, synthesising, evaluating, concluding, generalising, and justifying.
The TIMSS questions were not evenly spread across the content and cognitive domains, as shown in Exhibit 
3.1. As TIMSS assesses a range of problem-solving situations within mathematics, just under two-thirds of 
the items required students to use applying and reasoning skills. This distribution was consistent with the 
approach used for the New Zealand Curriculum. As Exhibit 3.1 shows, there was more emphasis on number 
questions, with the least emphasis on data display questions. Note that introductory or pre-algebra topics 
were included as part of the number domain.

Mathematics performance in relation to the content and 
cognitive domains
Achievement scores were created for the content and cognitive domains to provide a value so that they 
are comparable with each other. In the early cycles of TIMSS (prior to 2006), a score of 500 on one domain 
was not directly comparable to 500 on another domain. So a new methodology was created and applied to 
questions from the 2006/07 assessment onwards to ensure that these domains could be compared with each 
other. Therefore, it is important to note that comparisons between domains should only be made between the 
2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15 cycles, but not with earlier cycles (Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013b). 
In TIMSS 2014/15, the pattern of performance of New Zealand Year 5 students on the content and cognitive 
domains closely resembled the outcomes from previous cycles. As Exhibit 3.2 shows, New Zealand students 
performed relatively better on data display questions, compared to number and geometric shapes and 
measures. Students also did better at tasks that required them to apply their knowledge (applying) and use 
their reasoning, and poorer at questions that required them to demonstrate their knowledge of mathematical 
concepts, procedures, and facts (knowing - see Exhibit 3.3). 
There was no consistent pattern among the high performing countries, although many of them seem to do 
better at number compared with the other content domains. In Singapore, number was a relative strength, 
whereas there was not much of a difference across the content domains in Hong Kong SAR and the 
Republic of Korea. 
With the exception of Hong Kong SAR, most of the high-achieving countries had knowing as the highest of 
the three cognitive domains (see Exhibit 3.3). In Hong Kong SAR, knowing and applying were higher relative 
to reasoning.

Changes over time in content and cognitive domains
As Exhibit 3.4 shows, between 2006/07 and 2010/11, there was a significant decrease in the scores for 
geometric shapes and measures (12 points) and data display (15 points). Performance on data display questions 
has significantly improved since 2011 (15 points), back to where it was in 2006. In contrast, the changes in the 
mean scale scores for the number and geometric shapes and measures domains were not significant.
New Zealand Year 5 students saw a significant increase across the mean scale scores for the applying 
(7 points) and reasoning (13 points) cognitive domains in 2014/15 compared to 2010/11 bringing it back to 
the level it was in 2006/07. The achievement for the knowing domain stayed similar to 2010/11 which was 
significantly lower than the achievement in 2006/07.

Singapore and Hong Kong SAR, the highest performing countries, saw a significant increase in the mean

TIMSS and the New Zealand 
mathematics curriculum
CHAPTER 4:
 Key Findings
Many New Zealand Year 5 students were working at level 2 of the curriculum by the end of the 
year, rather than level 3. Those students working at higher levels had higher achievement.
When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for New Zealand Year 5 
students, there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when 
analysis reduced the test to contain only those appropriate to New Zealand Year 5 students, 
the average student got less than half the items correct.
New Zealand Year 5 students did best on data display questions, the area of the curriculum 
most likely to be covered by their teachers.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
TIMSS examines all aspects of curriculum: the intended curriculum, the implemented 
curriculum, and the attained curriculum. In New Zealand, the New Zealand 
Curriculum (NZC) lays out what is intended to be implemented and attained, and this 
guides teaching and learning. 
In the NZC, the alignment of curriculum levels with year levels is flexible. Teachers 
are expected to tailor lessons to meet students’ individual needs (the implemented 
curriculum). Students in the same year level may be working at different curriculum 
levels, as appropriate to their abilities and pace of progression. 
As in previous cycles of TIMSS, teachers were asked to indicate which topics they had 
taught to their Year 5 students in the current school year (implemented curriculum), 
and the curriculum level the majority of their Year 5 students were working at (the 
attained curriculum). This section will examine the implementation of the curriculum, 
as well as the match between the TIMSS test and the intended curriculum.
Mathematics curriculum levels and the TIMSS content 
domains
According to the New Zealand Curriculum Mathematics Standards, by the end of their fifth year of schooling, 
students will be achieving at early level 3 in the Mathematics and Statistics learning area of the curriculum 
(Ministry of Education, 2009). New Zealand teachers were asked at which level(s) of Mathematics and 
Statistics in the New Zealand Curriculum most of their Year 5 students were working for each of the strands: 
number and algebra, geometry and measurement, and statistics. Exhibit 4.1 shows that the proportion of 
students working at level 3 of the curriculum varied across strands, and that many classes were working 
across levels of the curriculum. Just under a third of students were in classes where the majority of students 
were working at level 3 of the curriculum on the number and algebra strand (31%), but only one-quarter on 
the other two strands. More than half of the classes were working across levels 2 and 3. Results from the 
National Monitoring Study of Student Achievement (Educational Assessment Research Unit & New Zealand 
Council for Educational Research, 2015) also show many students working at lower levels of the curriculum 
than intended.
We can use the TIMSS content domains to examine attainment on the curriculum strands due to their 
similar mathematical content. For example the number content domain for middle primary in TIMSS 
consists of understandings and skills related to three topic areas: whole numbers; fractions and decimals; 
and expressions, simple equations, and relationships (see Gronmo, Lindquist, Arora, & Mullis, 2013). Similarly 
the number and algebra strand at level 3 of the NZC consists of: number strategies, number knowledge 
(which includes fractions and decimals), equations and expressions, and patterns and relations (Ministry of 
Education, 2007). 
Exhibit 4.1 shows that students whose classes were working at higher levels of the curriculum had higher 
achievement on the associated TIMSS content domain. Examining these results in the international context 
shows that if we had only included those Year 5 students working at level 3 of the curriculum in the 
TIMSS testing, New Zealand would still have had a lower mean score than the high- performing countries 
(Singapore, the Republic of Korea, Hong Kong SAR, Chinese Taipei, and Japan). For example, the average 
score for New Zealand students working at level 3 of the statistics strand of the curriculum is still significantly 
lower on the corresponding data display content domain (529) than their Singaporean counterparts (600 
scale score points).

Curriculum match
Questions about international studies often focus on the appropriateness of the assessment questions 
for New Zealand students. New Zealand is not unique in asking this question; other countries are also 
concerned with the appropriateness of these tests. The TIMSS assessment questions are developed through 
a collaborative process that begins with the development of an assessment framework. The TIMSS 2015 
Mathematics Framework (Gronmo, Lindquist, Arora, & Mullis, 2013) was designed to specify the important 
aspects of mathematics that participating countries agreed should be the focus of an international 
assessment of mathematics achievement. However it is inevitable that the tests included questions that were 
unfamiliar to some students in some countries. In order to investigate the extent to which the TIMSS 2014/15 
assessment was relevant to each country’s curriculum, TIMSS conducted a Test-Curriculum Matching Analysis 
(TCMA). The TCMA was also used to investigate the impact of selecting only appropriate questions on a 
country’s performance.
For the TCMA, each assessment question was examined using the following two criteria:
»»
whether or not the topic of the question is in the intended curriculum for the majority (50% or more) of 
middle primary students in the grade or school level tested (in our case Year 5);
»»
whether or not the item topic was intended to be encountered by the students prior to the TIMSS 
testing (in our case October or November 2014).
All questions, regardless of this analysis, were included in any overall results reported for TIMSS. However, 
this analysis was used to ascertain the level to which the results might change for New Zealand if only 
questions judged appropriate for New Zealand students were included in the tests. The analysis also included 
an examination of how students in other countries would fare if they were only given the “New Zealand-
appropriate” test.
Exhibit 4.2 shows the proportion of questions considered appropriate to the New Zealand Curriculum in 
each of the TIMSS content areas. However, it should be noted that New Zealand’s mathematics curriculum 
provides some challenges for deciding whether or not at least half of Year 5 students are likely to have 
met the question topics in the TIMSS test. The curriculum is not prescriptive, instead providing some broad 
guidelines of mathematics concepts and skills that schools can choose to cover. Schools are encouraged 
to design mathematics programmes that are relevant to their students and communities. Consequently, 
when schools plan their mathematics programmes there is considerable variation between them. Another 
challenge is that the broad achievement objectives are grouped in levels that cover approximately two 
years of schooling. The National Standards in Mathematics were used in this analysis to give a clearer 
understanding of what teachers might be teaching their Year 5 students.
As shown in the previous section, New Zealand Year 5 students were generally working at levels 2 and 3 of 
the curriculum, so information from levels 1, 2, and 3 was used to guide judgements on the TCMA, along with 
stages 4, 5, and 6 of the National Standards. In addition, curriculum-matched resources available on nzmaths.
co.nz were used for further clarification (our thanks to the teachers who did this work).
Exhibit 4.2: Appropriateness of the TIMSS 2014/15 tests to the New Zealand Curriculum

As Exhibit 4.2 shows, 75 percent of the TIMSS 2014/15 questions were judged appropriate for 
New Zealand Year 5 students in terms of the curriculum expectations. However, the TCMA analysis shows 
that even if the TIMSS test was reduced to include only those questions considered appropriate to the 
New Zealand Curriculum, the average New Zealand Year 5 student would have got less than half the items 
correct (see Exhibit 4.3). In contrast, the average student in some of the high performing countries would 
have got around three-quarters of the items correct.

Coverage of mathematics topics
To ascertain the implemented curriculum, teachers of the selected students were asked what opportunities 
students had to learn the content in the TIMSS tests. Teachers provided information on mathematics topics 
taught to Year 5 students prior to, or during, the year of the TIMSS assessment. For each of 17 topics, 
teachers were asked if the topic was mostly taught before this year, mostly taught this year, or not yet taught 
or just introduced. These 17 topics cover the TIMSS 2015 Mathematics Framework (Gronmo, Lindquist, Arora, 
& Mullis, 2013). They provide a link between the TIMSS framework and the TIMSS assessment. Note that this 
question is about whether the topic was taught, but does not gather information on the depth or breadth of 
the teaching on the topic. For example, the topic of number patterns (extending number patterns and finding 
missing terms) might cover quite a variety of difficulties of patterns in one classroom, but only very simple 
ones in another classroom – this question does not gather that level of detail. 
Just over eight in every ten New Zealand Year 5 students had been taught all these topics in 2014 or the 
preceding years (82% of students). In comparison, just over three-quarters of middle primary students (76%) 
on average across other countries had been taught all of these 17 topics (ranging from 55% in Morocco to 
93% in Portugal – see Exhibit 9.3, Mullis, Martin, Foy, & Hooper, 2016).
More New Zealand Year 5 students had been taught data display topics in 2014 or the preceding years (93%) 
than number topics (87%) or geometric shapes and measures topics (74%). Note that this question was not 
about the proportion of time spent on these but rather whether the topic was covered or not. As mentioned 
earlier, data display is the area of TIMSS where New Zealand students show the best performance, while the 
other two areas were lower.

34
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
The mathematics topics covered by the fewest New Zealand Year 5 students were:
»»
comparing and drawing angles (40%);
»»
concepts of decimals, including place value and ordering, adding and subtracting with decimals (62%);
»»
using informal coordinate systems to locate points in a plane (67%).
Exhibit 4.4 shows a complete list of topics and coverage among New Zealand Year 5 students.
In the previous cycle of TIMSS in 2010/11, the topics of adding and subtracting with decimals, and adding 
and subtracting with fractions were among the least taught topics in New Zealand. Adding and subtracting 
decimals had the lowest coverage in 2011 (33%), but is now integrated with concepts of decimals, including 
place value and ordering. Adding and subtracting with fractions (38% in 2011) is now integrated with 
comparing and ordering fractions. 
New Zealand Year 5 students who were in classes where the topic was covered (the year of the assessment 
or in previous years) generally had higher achievement than those where the topic was not covered. 
However, Portugal, Northern Ireland, and Oman all had more than 90 percent coverage of the topics, but 
were not among the highest performing countries. In contrast, the highest performing countries, Singapore 
(85%), Hong Kong SAR (85%), and the Republic of Korea (73%), all had lower than 90 percent coverage of 
the topics.
Exhibit 4.4: Mathematics topics taught to Year 5 students in New Zealand before or during 2014
Topic
Proportion of Year 5 students in classes

Mathematics achievement 
of Year 5 boys and girls 
CHAPTER 5:
 Key Findings
Year 5 boys and girls had the same mathematics achievement on average, but boys had a wider 
range and more advanced achievers than girls.
Boys and girls in 2014 both had significantly higher mathematics achievement than 20 years ago.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
37
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
New Zealand has many observed differences in achievement across subjects between 
boys and girls, with girls generally outperforming boys (Chamberlain, 2011; New 
Zealand Qualifications Authority, 2011; New Zealand Qualifications Authority, 2014). 
This pattern of girls out-performing boys has also been observed in other countries, 
with the exception of mathematics and science, where in many cases boys achieve 
just as well as girls (Bramley, Vidal Rodeiro, & Vitello, 2015). A Ministry of Education 
synthesis (Ministry of Education, 2007) highlighted several issues around the 
achievement of boys which included:
»»
early problems in reading; 
»»
lower achievement in reading and writing; 
»»
disengagement with school; 
»»
lower qualification attainment.
The synthesis report was supported by a range of qualification and participation data 
showing different proportions of boys and girls having success. The previous cycle 
of TIMSS was the first to see a significant difference in mathematics achievement 
between New Zealand boys and girls, although only at the Year 9 level. Year 5 boys 
and girls, on the other hand, had the same mean mathematics score. This chapter will 
examine the mathematics achievement of Year 5 boys and girls in TIMSS in 2014/15, 
with some comparisons with previous cycles.

38
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
Mathematics achievement of boys and girls
The 2014/15 cycle of TIMSS found Year 5 girls and boys had a similar score in mathematics achievement (490 
and 492 respectively). The range of achievement for boys was wider than for girls, as shown in Exhibit 5.1. 
This pattern is consistent with previous cycles of TIMSS, where there was no significant difference in mean 
mathematics achievement, but a wider range of achievement for boys than for girls.
Since 1994/95, the range of achievement has generally been decreasing for both girls and boys, although 
2002/03 remains the narrowest for boys and 2010/11 the narrowest for girls. As Exhibit 5.1 shows, between 
2010/11 and 2014/15 the range increased for both girls and boys. For girls, the change was primarily at the top 
of the range, but for boys there was a change at both ends of the spectrum. 
Countries that were high-performing had no gender difference, with the exceptions of Hong Kong SAR, the 
Republic of Korea and Chinese Taipei that had a significant gender difference in favour of boys. Hong Kong 
SAR had the largest gender difference in favour of boys (10 scale score points).

Benchmarks for boys and girls

benchmarks were not significant. Also, there were relatively large proportions of both boys (17%) and girls

Achievement on the content and cognitive domains for boys 
and girls
Just as there were no overall differences in mean mathematics achievement between boys and girls, there 
were also no significant differences across content domains between boys and girls. This is in contrast to 
2010/11, where girls had significantly higher achievement on the data display questions compared with 
boys. In 2014/15, girls and boys saw a significant increases in the data display category compared to their 
counterparts in 2010/11. 
Girls have significantly improved on the reasoning cognitive domain from 2010/11. However, the knowing and 
applying cognitive domains have not changed significantly since 2010/11, as shown in Exhibit 5.5. Boys have 
not changed significantly on the cognitive domains between 2010/11 and 2014/15. Boys however, scored 
significantly higher than girls in the knowing cognitive domain in 2014/15.

Mathematics achievement, 
ethnicity, and language of 
the home of Year 5 students 
CHAPTER 6:
 Key Findings
Nearly one-quarter of all Year 5 students identified themselves as belonging in more than one 
ethnic grouping; the groupings were Māori, Pasifika, Pākehā/European, Asian, and Other.
There is a wide variation in mathematics achievement within ethnic groupings with advanced 
achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings.
Māori had lower average mathematics achievement than non-Māori. Similarly, Pasifika students 
had lower average mathematics achievement than non-Pasifika. There was evidence that this 
achievement gap narrows when socio-economic factors are taken into account.
Nearly one-third of Year 5 students regularly spoke a language other than English at home 
(most also spoke English).

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
43
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
In the four year plan of the Ministry of Education, one of its stated priorities is “Māori 
and Pasifika learning and success” (Ministry of Education, 2016). They state that 
“Equitable achievement for Māori, Pasifika and students from low socio-economic 
backgrounds is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of 
Education, 2016). While Māori and Pasifika are not a new focus, with previous 
documents and programmes aimed towards these students, it is important to review 
our progress towards this goal. Given that Māori and Pasifika will be an increasingly 
large proportion of New Zealand’s working age population in the future, improving 
the system so that more of them will achieve will contribute to the future wellbeing 
and prosperity of New Zealand (Ministry of Education, 2016).
It is important to interpret this data carefully. It is not the ethnicity of a student per 
se that influences outcomes. However there is clear evidence that when teaching is 
culturally responsive, Māori students, and those of other minority ethnic groupings, thrive 
at school (Bishop et al., 2007). In contrast, Blank, Houkamau, & Kingi (2016) suggest that 
the unconscious bias of teachers lowers their expectations of what Māori can achieve. 
Thus the existence of a relationship between ethnicity and achievement, demonstrated in 
this section, does not imply that being classified in a particular ethnic grouping is a cause 
of poor or good achievement. In contrast, language knowledge may strongly influence 
mathematics learning with languages like Māori and some of the Asian languages being 
inherently structured for an understanding of base ten and place value. 
This section will examine mathematics achievement among ethnic groupings and 
language users. Students were able to identify with more than one ethnic grouping, 
and so results should be read with this overlap in mind. Also note that only English-
medium students are included in this cycle of TIMSS.

44
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
Classifying students by ethnicity
Students were asked about their ethnicity by asking “are you...?” and then listing the major ethnic groupings 
in New Zealand. The list also included an opportunity for the student to record any Other grouping they 
might identify with. In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand classified each student to one grouping only, 
using prioritisation. In this cycle, any student who identified with more than one grouping was assigned 
to each of those groupings, in line with the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classification (Statistics 
New Zealand, 2005). Students were then assigned to the following groupings based on their responses: 
Māori, Pasifika, Asian, Pākehā/European, and Other. If a student ticked both Māori and Samoan, they would 
be classified as both Māori and Pasifika. Using this methodology, ethnic groupings cannot be compared with 
each other as 22 percent of students are in more than one grouping (some are in as many as four groupings). 
Exhibit 6.1 shows how students were classified. There were proportionately more students in each ethnic 
grouping using this methodology than if we had used prioritisation.
Exhibit 6.1: Proportion of Year 5 students classified in each ethnic grouping in TIMSS 2014/15
Ethnic grouping
Proportion of Year 5 students

4

22
The next sections examine the mathematics achievement of students within each ethnic grouping, and 
comparisons are only made with those that are NOT in that ethnic grouping.
Mathematics achievement among Māori
As shown in Exhibit 6.2, there was a range of achievement among Māori Year 5 students. The difference 
between high and low achieving Māori was almost the same as non-Māori. However, the whole distribution 
for Māori was lower than for non-Māori, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of Māori was much 
lower than non-Māori. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS and other studies.

Mathematics achievement among Māori girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the mathematics achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Similarly, 
Māori girls had the same mathematics achievement as Māori boys. This finding was the same as in 2010/11.
Mathematics achievement among Māori students, taking into account socio-
economic status
Some preliminary analyses were conducted to see if taking into account the socio-economic status of 
students affected the achievement of Māori students. Fewer households are buying and storing lots of books 
as the popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases (see chapter 7). However, the number of books in 
the home still has a strong relationship with achievement. These preliminary analyses included only books in 
the home and home possessions, but show that when these measures of socio-economic status are taken into 
account, the difference in achievement between Māori and non-Māori is smaller (40 scale score points instead 
of 53). This finding suggests that ’being Māori’ does not explain the difference in achievement between Māori 
and non-Māori. Further exploration is warranted to understand the difference in achievement between Māori 
and non-Māori. An understanding of the barriers to learning can help break those barriers down so that Māori 
students can achieve to their true potential. Further exploration should also keep in mind the idea that Māori 
students face impediments to learning due to deficit thinking by teachers (see for example Blank et al., 2016).
Mathematics achievement among Pasifika
As shown in Exhibit 6.6, there was a range of achievement among Pasifika Year 5 students. The difference 
between high and low achieving Pasifika was a little smaller than non-Pasifika. However, the whole 
distribution for Pasifika was lower than for non-Pasifika, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of 
Pasifika was lower than non-Pasifika. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS and other studies.

Mathematics achievement among Pasifika girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the mathematics achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Similarly, 
Pasifika girls had the same mathematics achievement as Pasifika boys.
Mathematics achievement among Pasifika students, taking into account socio-
economic status
Some preliminary analyses were conducted to see if taking into account the socio-economic status of 
students affected the achievement of Pasifika students. Fewer households are buying and storing lots of 
books as the popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases (see chapter 7). However, the number of 
books in the home still has a strong relationship with achievement. These preliminary analyses included only 
books in the home and home possessions, but show that when these measures of socio-economic status 
are taken into account, the difference in achievement between Pasifika and non-Pasifika is smaller 
(40 scale score points instead of 60). This finding suggests that ’being Pasifika’ does not explain the 
difference in achievement between Pasifika and non-Pasifika. Further exploration is warranted to understand 
the difference in achievement between Pasifika and non-Pasifika. An understanding of the barriers to 
learning can help break those barriers down so that Pasifika students can achieve to their true potential. 
Further exploration should also keep in mind the idea that Pasifika students face impediments to learning 
due to deficit thinking by teachers (Bishop et al., 2007).
Mathematics achievement among Pākehā/European
As shown in Exhibit 6.10, there was a range of achievement among Pākehā/European Year 5 students. 
The difference between high and low achieving Pākehā/European was smaller than non-Pākehā/European. 
However, the whole distribution for Pākehā/European Year 5 students was higher than for non-Pākehā/
European, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of Pākehā/European is higher than non-Pākehā/
European. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS and other studies.

Mathematics achievement by language
Students were asked how often they spoke the language of instruction at home. In New Zealand, TIMSS 
was only tested in schools where English was the main medium of instruction. This question was formulated 
differently from previous cycles, so no comparisons can be made with these previous cycles. More than 
two-thirds of Year 5 students (69%) indicated that they always spoke English at home, and another 15 
percent almost always spoke English at home. Nearly all of the rest of the Year 5 students indicated that 
they sometimes spoke English and sometimes another language (14%). Less than two percent of students 
reported never speaking English at home. Those students who spoke another language, at least a little, most 
commonly spoke an Asian language or Māori every day at home. 
Those Year 5 students who sometimes or never spoke English at home most commonly spoke an Asian 
language or a Pasifika language, as shown in Exhibit 6.22. 
Exhibit 6.22: Languages spoken by Year 5 students who sometimes or never spoke English at home in 
TIMSS 2014/15
Always or
almost always
spoke English
Sometimes or
never spoke
English
16%
84%
2 or more, 4%
European, 11%
Māori, 14%
Pasifika, 21%
Asian, 43%
Other, 7%
 
Many studies point to the advantages of bilingualism (or indeed multilingualism), including greater 
flexibility of thinking (see for example Adesope, Lavin, Thompson, & Ungerleider, 2010). The base 10 
structure of languages such as Māori and Chinese may also be an advantage for students learning to add 
and subtract. The relationship between language used at home and average mathematics achievement in 
the English-medium TIMSS test was complex. It appears that speaking another language at home could 
be advantageous, as those who almost always spoke English at home and spoke some words of another 
language had the highest achievement. Because the mathematics questions in TIMSS include many items 
that require students to first read the question and work out which mathematics is required, a good facility 
with English is required. Therefore to fully understand the relationship between being bilingual, or multi-
lingual, and mathematics achievement, we need to know how well students understand each language.

Mathematics achievement 
and socio-economic status 
of Year 5 students 
CHAPTER 7:
 Key Findings
Children in homes with many resources for learning had higher mathematics achievement, on 
average, than those whose homes had fewer resources.
Mathematics achievement was higher, on average, for Year 5 students in schools with more 
affluent students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The 
difference in mathematics achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was 
higher than most other countries in the TIMSS study.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
57
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
The Ministry of Education’s four year plan from 2016 to 2020 states that “Equitable 
achievement for Māori, Pasifika and students from low socio-economic backgrounds 
is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
Results from the large-scale international studies, PIRLS, TIMSS, and PISA1, have 
all shown New Zealand has a large disparity in achievement between students with 
high socio-economic status (high SES) and those with low socio-economic status 
(low SES). Schools in New Zealand have received differential funding, based on the 
needs of the communities they are situated in, since TIMSS first began (in 1994), yet 
disparities persist. Therefore, it is important to continue to examine the relationship 
between socio-economic status and achievement of students. This chapter will 
present details of some measures used to examine socio-economic status, along with 
their association with mathematics achievement.
Possessions and books as measures of socio-economic 
status of students
Home possessions and books in the home can be used to give a measure of both the wealth of the home 
and the level of importance given to education and culture. The TIMSS questionnaires asked middle primary 
students about the presence in their home of books and a series of other resources which could be used to 
gauge SES. Arguably some of these resources could be considered facilitators of educational activities, such 
as an internet connection, but might not necessarily be used as such. This next section will discuss the results 
of these questions.
Books in the home
TIMSS has asked about the number of books in the home since 1994/95. In 2002/03, the question was 
changed slightly to include pictures of what each of the five categories might look like (see Exhibit 7.1). 
Exhibit 7.2 shows the proportions of students in three summarised categories of numbers of books in the 
home, and their average (mean) mathematics achievement.
Exhibit 7.1: Images middle primary students were shown to identify the number of books in their home 
 
Note: 	
This exhibit has been adapted for this report. It does not show the questionnaire options, nor does it show one of the five 
categories (more than 200 books). 
1	
PIRLS is the Progress in International Reading Literacy Study and has been conducted in 2001, 2005, 2010, & 2015 in New 
Zealand – see http://www.educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pirls. PISA is the Programme for International Student 
Assessment and has been conducted in 2000, 2003, 2006, 2009, 2012, & 2015 in New Zealand – see http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa.

Just under one-third of New Zealand Year 5 students (30%) reported that they had 25 or fewer books in 
their home. Just over one-third of New Zealand Year 5 students (36%) reported that they had more than 
100 books in their home. This proportion of students with more than 100 books is the same as 2010/11, but 
much lower than in 1994/95 (62% - as mentioned earlier, there was the same wording in 1994/95, but no 
pictures with the question). It seems likely that fewer households are buying and storing lots of books as the 
popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases.
As shown in Exhibit 7.2, Year 5 students that reported more books in the home had significantly higher 
mathematics achievement, on average, than those with fewer books. This implies that despite fewer 
households buying and storing lots of books, the number of books in the home still has a strong relationship 
with achievement.

More Pākehā/European Year 5 students had many books in their home (more than 100 books – 44%) than 
non-Pākehā/European students (20%). Book ownership was less common among Year 5 students in the 
Pasifika (21% had more than 100 books), Asian (26% had more than 100 books) and Māori (29% had more 
than 100 books) groupings. Despite there being fewer students in some ethnic groupings with more than 100 
books, the strong relationship between books in the home and mathematics achievement was still evident 
within each ethnic grouping.
Resources in the home
Exhibit 7.3 shows the proportions of Year 5 students that had each of the listed resources in their homes. 
Nearly all students reported having a computer in their home (96% had either a computer of their own or a 
computer they shared with others in their family), and the majority of students had an internet connection 
(89%). Note that adults who supervised the questionnaire administration regularly reported receiving queries 
about how to answer this question from students who lived in more than one house, as part of a shared 
custody arrangement.

The relationship between the home possessions and mathematics achievement was evident among all 
ethnic groupings. However, far fewer Pasifika Year 5 students (14%) had nearly all the home possessions 
(12 to 14) compared with non-Pasifika (24%). Nearly one-fifth of Asian students (19%), nearly one-quarter 
of Māori students (22%), and just over one-quarter of Pakeha/European students (26%) had nearly all the 
home possessions.
Home resources for learning
To further understand the relationship between home resources and student achievement, the international 
researchers combined the responses from several questions asked of the Year 5 students and parents. These 
questions included the: 
»»
number of books in the home (students); 
»»
number of children’s books in the home (parents); 
»»
number of study supports (internet connection or own room – students); 
»»
highest level of education of either parent (parents); 
»»
highest level of occupation of either parent (parents). 
Although this data should be read with caution as only 56 percent of New Zealand parents returned their 
questionnaire, it is still possible to illustrate the trend in achievement. Note that New Zealand stands out in 
the international report for having one of the highest proportions of middle primary students coming from 
well resourced homes – but this is tempered by the knowledge that only three other countries had fewer 
than 70 percent of parents responding to the questionnaire; all other countries had higher response rates. 
Children in homes with many resources for learning had higher mathematics achievement, on average, than 
those whose homes had fewer resources, as shown in Exhibit 7.5.

Socio-economic indicators of schools attended
Schools with larger numbers of students from low socio-economic communities are provided with extra 
funding per student. The decile indicator within New Zealand is used to allocate differentiated funding. Decile 
1 schools are the schools with the highest proportion of students from socio-economically disadvantaged 
communities, while decile 10 schools have the lowest proportion of students from these communities. 
Internationally, there was also information collected from principals that allowed examination of the socio-
economic status of the school intake. This section will examine these differing measures of socio-economic 
status: decile, and composition by student economic background.
Decile
Previous cycles of TIMSS have shown that students attending schools with fewer students from lower socio-
economic backgrounds (having higher decile) had higher average mathematics achievement than those 
attending schools with more students from lower socio-economic backgrounds (from lower decile schools – 
see Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013). As shown in Exhibit 7.6, this was also true of the latest cycle of TIMSS, 
with students from higher decile schools (9 and 10) having higher average mathematics achievement (527 
scale score points) than those from the low decile schools (1 and 2 – 418 scale score points).

data, as presented in the international reports.

There were high achieving Year 5 students in all decile groupings, as measured by the high and advanced

schools achieving at or above the high benchmarks (39% in decile 9 and 10 schools) compared with the 
lower decile schools (6% in decile 1 and 2 schools). As shown in Exhibit 7.7, there were Year 5 students in 
all decile groupings who did not demonstrate the ability to complete a reasonable number of the simplest 
mathematics tasks which TIMSS seeks to measure (that is they correctly completed less than half of the low 
benchmark tasks). However, there were larger proportions of students in the lower decile groupings in this 
very low achieving group (41% of decile 1 and 2 students below low) compared with high decile groupings 
(6% of decile 9 and 10 students).

School composition by student economic background
To make international comparisons across schools, there was also information collected from principals 
across the TIMSS countries that allowed examination of the socio-economic status of the combined student 
population in the schools. The School Questionnaire included two questions, one that asked about the 
approximate proportions of students in the school from economically disadvantaged homes, and one 
that asked about the approximate proportions from economically affluent homes. Principals were asked 
to choose from four categories: 0 to 10%, 11 to 25%, 26 to 50%, and more than 50%. The international 
researchers combined the responses from these two questions to give a measure of school composition by 
student economic background. The measure created was an index with three categories: schools with more 
affluent than disadvantaged students; schools with neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged students; 
and schools with more disadvantaged than affluent students. This measure allows us to compare the equity 
of our system with other countries.
As shown in Exhibit 7.8, nearly half of New Zealand Year 5 students (47%) were in schools with more affluent 
students, while just over one-quarter (27%) were in schools with more disadvantaged students. Average 
mathematics achievement was higher for Year 5 students in schools with more affluent students (521 scale 
score points) than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students (442 scale score points). 
The difference in mathematics achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand (79 scale 
score points) was higher than nearly all other countries in the TIMSS study; only South Africa had a higher 
difference (165 scale score points) than New Zealand. On average internationally, this difference was only 44 
scale score points. Australia (72 scale score points) and the United States (66 scale score points), also had 
relatively large differences between the middle primary students in more affluent schools and those in more 
economically disadvantaged schools.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

This large difference in average mathematics achievement between middle primary students in schools with 
more affluent students compared with schools with more disadvantaged students was consistent with TIMSS 
2010/11, and with reading achievement in PIRLS 2010/11 (Progress in International Reading Literacy Study - 
see Chamberlain, 2013).

Schools with more affluent than disadvantaged students are defined as those where the principal estimated that 25% or

As with the measure of decile, the TIMSS benchmarks show that there are high and low achievers within 
each of these three categories of economic composition of the school population. Exhibit 7.9 presents two 
different ways of looking at this data – those Year 5 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown 
in the graphical part) and those Year 5 students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in 
the table part).
There were larger proportions of Year 5 students in the more affluent schools achieving at or above the high 
benchmarks (36%) compared with the economically disadvantaged schools (11%). As shown in Exhibit 7.9, 
there were Year 5 students in each of the three categories of school composition who did not demonstrate 
the ability to complete a reasonable number of the simplest mathematics tasks which TIMSS seeks to 
measure (that is they did not reach the low benchmark). However, there were larger proportions of Year 5 
students in the economically disadvantaged schools in this low achieving group (32%) compared with more 
affluent schools (7%).

the schools with predominantly disadvantaged students face many challenges when providing education 
for these students. For example, these schools generally had a higher turnover of students (students leaving 
before the end of the year or transferring in part way through the year) and also were more likely to provide
