Home climate for Year 
5 students learning 
mathematics 
CHAPTER 8:
 Key Findings
Students who often did early literacy and numeracy learning activities with their parents 
(according to the parents) had higher mathematics achievement, on average, than students 
who did so less often.
Parental attitudes towards mathematics and science did not seem to have an effect on Year 5 
students’ mathematics achievement in the TIMSS assessment.
The average (mean) mathematics achievement score was higher for the group of Year 5 
students who read every day or almost every day, and declined as the amount of time spent on 
reading reduced.
New Zealand Year 5 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity compared 
to other activities which were culturally focussed.
Tutoring in mathematics was less common in New Zealand than among high-performing 
countries.

66
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
Activities at home prior to school entry 
In the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment, parents or caregivers of middle primary students were asked how often 
they engaged with the child in their pre-primary years on a variety of activities. Note that only 56 percent 
of New Zealand Year 5 parents returned the questionnaire, so values refer to only these students and their 
parents. There were 16 activities in total and they included: 
»»
reading books;
»»
playing word games; 
»»
telling stories;
»»
playing with number toys;
»»
reading aloud signs and labels; 
»»
playing games involving shapes;
»»
singing songs;
»»
talking about the things they did;
»»
saying counting rhymes or singing counting 
songs;
»»
talking about what they had read;
»»
playing with alphabet toys;
»»
writing letters or words;
»»
playing with building blocks or construction 
toys;
»»
counting different things; 
»»
playing board or card games;
»»
writing numbers.
To summarise the responses to these 16 activities, the international researchers created the Early literacy 
and numeracy activities scale, based on reported engagement from parents or caregivers with these 
activities. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories; often, 
sometimes, and never or almost never engaged.
There is a growing body of evidence linking informal learning activities in the home 
to the development of mathematics knowledge in children (Ramani & Siegler, 2015; 
Jordan, Kaplan, Ramineni, & Locuniak, 2009; LeFevre, Skwarchuk, Smith-Chant, Fast, 
Kamawar, & Bisanz, 2009). These learning activities or experiences appear to have the 
strongest effect in the early years of a child’s development before attending school.
Missall, Hojnoski, Caskie, & Repasky (2015) found a relationship between activities 
in the home and parent-reported mathematics beliefs. However, they did not 
find statistically significant relationships between children’s early mathematics 
performance and parental beliefs and behaviours towards mathematics. Soni & Kumari 
(2015), on the other hand, found gender differences for 10 to 15 year olds where the 
father’s attitude to mathematics positively influenced their son’s achievement and 
attitude towards mathematics, and the mother’s attitude had no effect. In contrast, the 
mother’s attitude to mathematics positively influenced their daughter’s achievement 
and attitude towards mathematics, and the father’s attitude had no effect.
This chapter will focus on interactional activities with parents, parental attitudes to 
mathematics, reading for enjoyment, leisure activities and tutoring outside of school, 
which may have an effect on student outcomes.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
67
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
On average across TIMSS countries, students whose parents reported often spending time with them had 
higher mathematics achievement than students whose parents did so only sometimes or almost never. 
This is also true for New Zealand where 61 percent of students had parents who reported that they often 
engaged with their child in early literacy and numeracy activities, 38 percent of students had parents 
sometimes engaged with their child, and only one percent never or almost never did. 
As shown in Exhibit 8.1, high performing countries like Singapore, Chinese Taipei and the Republic of Korea had 
a smaller proportion of students who were often engaged with these activities, compared to New Zealand. 
Therefore these high performing countries had higher proportions of parents sometimes or never or almost 
never engaging the student with these activities. The students in higher performing countries whose parents 
never or almost never engaged with them still performed very well.

Parental attitude to mathematics 
In addition to gauging how often parents or caregivers engaged with the child on learning activities, it is 
useful to understand their attitudes towards mathematics. In the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment, parents or 
caregivers responded to eight statements about their feelings towards mathematics and science, which were 
used to create scores for students on the Parental attitude toward mathematics and science scale. To report 
the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories: very positive attitude; positive 
attitude; and less than positive attitude.
As shown in Exhibit 8.2, parents or caregivers of 70 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were 
categorised as having a very positive attitude towards mathematics and science, which is higher than the 
international average. Only two percent had a less than positive attitude, while 28 percent of students had 
parents or caregivers categorised as having a positive attitude. 

68
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
The relationship between mathematics achievement and parent or caregiver attitudes within New Zealand 
differed from that observed on average internationally. Higher achievement scores in mathematics were 
observed for New Zealand Year 5 students if parents or caregivers had a very positive attitude towards 
mathematics and science, compared with just having a positive attitude. As there were very few students 
with parents or caregivers with a less than positive attitude towards mathematics and science, it was not 
possible to estimate a mean mathematics achievement score for this grouping. In contrast, although a 
lower achievement score was observed for middle primary students whose parents had a positive attitude, 
compared with a very positive attitude, the international average was the same for parents who had a 
very positive attitude and a less than positive attitude (see Exhibit 8.2). These results suggest that parental 
or caregiver attitudes may not necessarily affect achievement outcomes for middle primary students in 
mathematics and science, which is supported by Missall et al.’s (2015) findings. Future analysis on the 
gender effects of parent or caregiver attitudes towards mathematics and science on student mathematics 
achievement could be carried out to see if it supports the research recently done by Soni & Kumari (2015).

scores for New Zealand Year 5 students compared to the international average in TIMSS 2014/15

science (18%). They also had the smallest proportion of students whose parents or caregivers had a very 
positive attitude towards mathematics and science (14%).

Reading behaviour of Year 5 students and their parents
Parents were asked how often they spent reading for enjoyment each week. As shown in Exhibit 8.3, there 
was a consistent pattern across countries showing a higher proportion of parents or caregivers reading 
one to five hours each week than any other frequency. Also, increased hours spent by parents or caregivers 
reading each week was related to higher mean achievement scores in mathematics for middle primary 
students. A small exemption is observed for Hong Kong SAR and Chinese Taipei, where there was no 
difference in mathematics achievement between reading for six to ten hours and reading for more than ten 
hours. Compared to the high performing countries and the international average, a higher proportion of 
New Zealand parents or caregivers reported reading more than ten hours per week.

New Zealand Year 5 students were also asked how often they read for enjoyment to gauge their reading 
habits. Reading resources included books, e-books, comics, blogs, webpages and other electronic media. 
Note that a different frequency scale was used for students compared to parents. Around 65 percent of 
Year 5 students reported that they read every day or almost every day and 25 percent read once or twice a 
week. Only five percent read once or twice a month and four percent of students reported never or almost 
never reading for enjoyment (see Exhibit 8.4). Students that read every day or almost every day had higher 
mathematics scores on average. Mean mathematics scores decreased significantly as the amount of time 
students spend reading decreased.

Tutoring in mathematics outside school 
Year 5 parents or caregivers were asked if their child attended extra lessons or tutoring outside school, and 
whether they attended to excel in class or to keep up in class. As Exhibit 8.7 shows, high performing countries 
reported a higher proportion of students attending tutorials outside of school compared to New Zealand and 
the international average. These high performing countries were also more likely to report that the reason for 
attending tutorials was to keep up rather than excel in class. New Zealand students who attended tutorials 
to excel in class did better on average than those who attended to keep up in class and those who did not 
attend tutorials. Apart from Iran and Qatar, the mean achievement scores for middle primary students whose 
parents reported that their child attended tutorials to excel in class performed better on average compared to 
those who attended to keep up. Also, the mathematics achievement score, on average, for most countries was 
higher for those who did not attend tutorials compared to the groups that did.

Year 5 student attitudes 
to and engagement 
with mathematics 
CHAPTER 9:
 Key Findings
More than three quarters of New Zealand Year 5 students liked learning mathematics. However, 
compared to the international average, New Zealand Year 5 students were a bit more negative 
about mathematics.
While the majority of New Zealand Year 5 students were confident in mathematics, almost one 
third were not confident. New Zealand had more not confident and less very confident students 
in mathematics, compared with international averages.
Students had higher mathematics achievement, on average, if they were confident in their 
mathematics ability, or liked mathematics.
The mathematics achievement of Year 5 students had a stronger relationship with their 
confidence in mathematics than how much they liked learning mathematics.
More New Zealand Year 5 boys were very confident in mathematics, while more Year 5 girls 
were not confident. This also held across all ethnic groupings, except for Māori. Māori Year 5 
girls and boys had very similar proportions, both for liking and confidence.
The Pasifika grouping stood out with a bigger proportion (more than half) of students very 
much liking to learn mathematics, while the Pākehā/European grouping had almost 30 percent 
of students not liking to learn mathematics. 

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
75
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
Besides playing an indispensible role in technological advances, “mathematics 
and statistics have a broad range of practical applications in everyday life, in other 
learning areas, and in workplaces” (Ministry of Education, 2007). According to 
Gronmo et al. (2013), “learning mathematics improves problem-solving skills, and 
working through problems can teach persistence and perseverance”. 
It seems to be widely assumed that a desire to learn mathematics may have links 
with students’ confidence in, beliefs and attitudes towards mathematics, as well 
as their mathematical achievement (Peters & Kortecamp, 2010; Grootenboer 
& Marshman, 2015; Bonne, 2016; McDonough & Sullivan, 2014). In this regard, 
understanding the influence of these factors on students’ learning and achievement 
seems important, and may lead to shaping the practices that support confident, 
connected, actively involved and lifelong learners, as the New Zealand Curriculum 
envisions. It may also help to increase the percentage of students choosing to pursue 
mathematics related majors as their future career. 
This chapter looks at students’ attitudes towards learning mathematics, and their 
achievement in the context of how much they like mathematics and how confident 
they are in mathematics.
Student attitudes toward mathematics
To gauge their enjoyment and confidence, students were asked how much they agreed with a series of 
eighteen statements about learning mathematics. Half of the questions were about how much students liked 
learning mathematics, and the other half were about how confident they were in mathematics. Students 
were given four response options: agree a lot, agree a little, disagree a little, and disagree a lot.
As we can see from the Exhibit 9.1, New Zealand Year 5 students were generally positive about mathematics. 
Big proportions of students agreed (a lot and a little combined) that they learn many interesting things in 
maths and they enjoy learning maths (87% and 83% respectively). Also, 76 percent of students disagreed 
(a little and a lot combined) with the negative statement that they wished they didn’t have to learn maths. 
However, as a taught subject, maths was not one of the favourites for more than one third of students (35%), 
with a further 31 percent not looking forward to maths lessons and 30 percent not liking any schoolwork that 
involved numbers. 
In line with liking to learn mathematics, the majority of New Zealand Year 5 students were also confident, 
with 87 percent of the students agreeing that they usually do well in maths, and 75 percent learn things 
quickly in maths. But also, 47 percent of students agreed that maths is harder for them than for many of their 
classmates and 40 percent agreed that maths was harder for them than any other subjects.
The TIMSS 2014/15 study asked two new negative statements about students’ confidence in mathematics 
- maths makes me nervous and maths makes me confused. Both these statements had relatively big 
proportions of students agreeing with them (40% and 44% respectively).

Twelve of the statements were included in both 2010/11 and 2014/15 TIMSS studies. The percentages of 
students agreeing a lot, agreeing a little and disagreeing with the statements differed very little over this 
time.
Generally, Year 5 students with positive attitudes towards mathematics had higher achievement than 
students with less positive attitudes. The mathematics achievement of students had a stronger relationship 
with their confidence in mathematics than how much they liked learning mathematics. This relationship is 
further explored in the following sections. In order to examine students’ attitudes towards mathematics, the 
international researchers combined the data onto two scales: the Students like learning mathematics (SLM) 
and the Students confident in mathematics (SCM) scales. For ease of interpretation, each scale was then 
divided into three categories, according to the degree of agreement with the statements. 
Students like learning mathematics
The Students like learning mathematics scale included the following nine statements:
»»

»»

maths is boring;

»»

»»

»»

»»

»»
maths is one of my favourite subjects.

TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
Middle primary students were scored according to their degree of agreement with the statements, and the 
results were then categorised into one of three groups; those who very much like learning mathematics, 
those who like learning mathematics, and those who do not like learning mathematics. Note that TIMSS 
2010/11 analysis used different scale categories - like learning mathematics, somewhat like learning 
mathematics, and do not like learning mathematics (Mullis et al., 2012, Exhibit 8.1).
The proportion of New Zealand Year 5 students who very much liked learning mathematics was higher 
than those who liked learning mathematics and who did not like learning mathematics (see Exhibit 9.2).  
Compared to international average proportions, New Zealand Year 5 students were a bit more negative. 
Although there was a similar proportion of Year 5 students who liked learning, the percentage of those who 
very much liked learning mathematics in New Zealand was slightly less than the international average and, 
those who did not like learning mathematics was larger than international average.
As shown in Exhibit 9.2, the New Zealand Year 5 students who very much liked learning mathematics had a 
higher mean achievement score (498) than those in the other categories (488 for those who liked learning 
mathematics, and 485 for those who did not like learning mathematics). However, the difference between 
those who very much liked and those who did not like learning mathematics was smaller in New Zealand than 
that found on average internationally (13 scale score points compared to 38 scale score points internationally). 
The difference was smaller for New Zealand students because the mean mathematics achievement of those 
who very much liked learning mathematics was much lower than their international peers (498 compared to 
521 for the international average), while those who did not like learning mathematics had similar scores (485 
for NZ and 483 for the international average).

Although the international researchers have changed the scale statements and categories since the previous 
cycle, they have calculated an equivalent average scale score that allows us to compare TIMSS 2010/11 
and 2014/15 results. The New Zealand Year 5 students had an average scale score of 9.8 in 2014/15, which 
corresponds to like learning mathematics. This score is not significantly different from 9.9 in 2010/11 (Mullis et 
al., 2016, Exhibit 10.3).
Students’ confidence in mathematics
The Students confident in mathematics scale included the following nine statements:
»»
I usually do well in maths;
»»
maths is harder for me than for many of my classmates;
»»
I am just not good at maths;
»»
I learn things quickly in maths;
»»
maths makes me nervous;
»»
I am good at working out difficult maths problems;
»»
my teacher tells me I am good at maths;
»»
maths is harder for me than any other subject;
»»
maths makes me confused.
The students then were categorised into one of three groups, very confident, confident or not confident 
in mathematics, according to their responses to the statements. Note that the TIMSS 2010/11 study used 
different scale categories – students who were confident, somewhat confident and not confident in 
mathematics (Mullis et al., 2012, Exhibit 8.4).
As shown in Exhibit 9.3, almost half of New Zealand Year 5 students were confident in mathematics, while 22 
percent were very confident, and the remaining 30 percent were not confident in mathematics. New Zealand 
had a smaller proportion of very confident Year 5 students, and a greater proportion of not confident Year 5 
students, compared with international averages. 
Students who were more confident about their abilities to learn mathematics had higher mean achievement 
than those who were less confident – a relatively large difference of 91 scale score points for New Zealand 
Year 5 students. This scale is different from the liking mathematics scale (SLM), which had a much smaller (13 
scale score point) difference in achievement for New Zealand Year 5 students between those who very much 
like mathematics and those who do not like it.
Many of the high-performing countries had quite low proportions of very confident middle primary students 
(Hong Kong SAR 19%, Singapore 19%, Chinese Taipei 15%, Japan 15% and Rep. of Korea 13%). However, within 
each country, those students who had the highest levels of confidence in their mathematics abilities had 
higher average achievement than those who were less confident.

Attitudes to mathematics by gender
On the Students like learning mathematics scale, more New Zealand Year 5 girls liked learning mathematics, 
and more Year 5 boys very much liked learning mathematics, while similar proportion of boys and girls did 
not like learning mathematics (see Exhibit 9.4).
How much they reported liking mathematics made no significant difference to the average achievement of 
New Zealand Year 5 girls, but Year 5 boys who very much liked learning mathematics had significantly higher 
scores than boys who were in the other categories. The difference between the New Zealand Year 5 girls 
and boys achievement scores was not significant for those students who did not like learning mathematics 
(490 for girls and 479 for boys) and liked learning mathematics (492 for girls and 483 for boys), however 
boys who very much liked learning mathematics achieved significantly higher scores than girls in the same 
category.

Attitudes to mathematics by ethnicity
When attitudes to mathematics were considered, there was a consistent pattern on the Students like learning 
mathematics scale among all ethnic groupings; those ethnic groupings who had higher proportions of 
students who very much liked learning mathematics also had lower proportions of students who did not like 
learning mathematics (see Exhibit 9.6). The Pasifika grouping (57%) had the bigger proportion of students 
who very much liked learning mathematics, while the Pākehā/European (27%) grouping had the bigger 
proportion of students who didn’t like learning mathematics. 
There was, however, no consistent pattern in the students liking to learn mathematics and their achievement 
scores. How much they liked learning mathematics made no significant difference to achievement of Māori, 
Pasifika and Other ethnic grouping Year 5 students. In contrast, Pākehā/European and Asian students 
who very much liked learning mathematics did better on average than those who did not like learning 
mathematics. Interestingly, Māori students who did not like learning mathematics achieved much higher 
scores, on average, than those who liked and very much liked learning mathematics.

On the Students confident in mathematics scale, within all ethnic groupings, the proportion of Year 5 
students who were confident (middle of the scale) was bigger than those who were on either end of the 
scale. Pākehā/European, Māori, Pasifika, and the Other ethnic grouping had more than one-quarter of 
their Year 5 students who were not confident, while fewer than one-quarter were very confident. Similar 
proportions of Asian students were not confident as were very confident. The Pākehā/European grouping 
had a higher proportion of not confident students (30%) compared to non-Pākehā/European, while the 
Asian grouping had a higher proportion of very confident students (27%) when compared to non-Asian 
students. The proportion of Māori students was similar to that of non-Māori students across all confidence 
levels (see Exhibit 9.7).

There were some interesting differences when Year 5 boys and girls were compared within ethnic groupings

Year 5 teachers of 
mathematics 
CHAPTER 10:
 Key Findings
More New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers with postgraduate qualifications in 2014/15 
compared to 2010/11 (27% compared to 19%).
About one-fifth of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who had also specialised 
in mathematics. Internationally, more students (41% on average) had teachers with a 
specialisation in mathematics.
New Zealand teachers felt better prepared to teach data display topics than number and 
geometric shapes and measures. The students whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach 
the mathematics topics had significantly higher achievement.
Most Year 5 students had teachers who expressed high or very high confidence in their own 
ability to teach mathematics. Of the statements listed, the most confidence was expressed 
in showing students a variety of problem solving strategies and the least confidence was 
expressed in developing students’ higher-order thinking skills.
Compared to most of the participating countries, more New Zealand teachers participated in 
mathematics related professional development activities. 

86
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
“To ensure every student achieves success our schooling system must provide 
high quality learning opportunities to meet the educational needs of all young 
New Zealanders”(Education Review Office, 2015). Within schools and kura, the 
quality of teaching has the biggest influence on student achievement (Ministry 
of Education, 2016). The New Zealand Government established the Education 
Council of Aotearoa New Zealand in 2015 as a new mandated professional body 
for teachers to lift the status of the teaching profession, and publicly recognise the 
role of quality teaching and educational leadership in raising student achievement. 
Among many tasks, the council is also mandated to develop standards for teachers 
(Ministry of Education, 2016).
Along with student achievement, the TIMSS study also collects information about 
teacher education, professional development and experiences. This data may not 
provide enough insight into the connection between student achievement and the 
former, as schools may have different strategies to make use of teaching practices 
and teachers’ expertise. However, it is useful to know if well-educated people are 
being attracted into teaching and whether they are being supported in their 
professional development, and if the schools are actively aiming to retain the 
highly experienced teachers. 
This chapter will report information collected about the characteristics of middle 
primary teachers, particularly focusing on their education, years of experience, 
preparedness and confidence to teach mathematics, and professional development 
they have undertaken. Note that this section will examine the mathematics 
teachers of middle primary students who were usually, but not always, the 
classroom teacher of the students. In some cases, classes were reorganised 
for mathematics lessons.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Experience
More than one-quarter of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who had 20 years or more teaching 
experience, and 18 percent had teachers with less than five years teaching experience (Exhibit 10.1). On 
average, the New Zealand Year 5 mathematics teachers had had 14 years of teaching experience. This has not 
changed significantly from 2010/2011, where the average experience of mathematics teachers was 13 years. 
On average internationally, a lot more middle primary students had mathematics teachers with 20 years or 
more teaching experience than New Zealand. The countries that had less experienced teachers than New 
Zealand were mostly the Arabic-speaking countries. England and Singapore were the only English-speaking 
countries that had less experienced teachers than New Zealand. Both countries had more students whose 
teachers had less than 5 years teaching experience (35% and 32% of students respectively, compared with 
18% in New Zealand). New Zealand Year 5 mathematics teachers had similar average experience as their 
Australian, Canadian, Irish and American counterparts. High achieving Asian countries like Japan, Republic of 
Korea and Chinese Taipei had more experienced teachers than New Zealand, while teachers from Hong Kong 
had similar average experience as New Zealand teachers. 
Teachers’ years of experience appeared to have no strong relationship with the achievement of students, 
both in New Zealand and internationally.

Education
As shown in Exhibit 10.2, more than one-quarter of New Zealand Year 5 students (27%) had teachers with 
a postgraduate university degree, and 58 percent had teachers with a bachelor’s degree or equivalent. 
Compared with previous the TIMSS cycle (19% of students in TIMSS 2010/11), more New Zealand Year 5 
students had teachers with postgraduate or higher degrees in 2014/15.
New Zealand had similar proportions to the international average. However, there were large variations across 
countries; a number of countries such as the Slovak Republic (100%), Poland (97%), the Czech Republic 
(94%) and Finland (90%) had a large proportion of teachers with postgraduate university degrees. On the 
contrary, there were quite a few countries including Hungary (99%), Belgium (Flemish – 98%), Japan (90%), 
Portugal (89%) and Norway (88%) where the large majority of students had teachers with a bachelor’s 
degree but no postgraduate degree.

Three-quarters of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who had trained as primary teachers but did 
not have a specialisation in mathematics and only about one-fifth (19%) of Year 5 students had teachers who 
had specialised in mathematics (see Exhibit 10.3). Internationally, more middle primary students (41%) had 
teachers with a specialisation in mathematics. Some countries, like Poland (100%), Kuwait (89%), Bahrain 
(89%), Jordan (87%), and Oman (87%), had more than 85 percent of their mathematics teachers who had 
specialised in mathematics.1

How well prepared do primary teachers feel?
To understand how well prepared the teachers felt to teach mathematics, TIMSS asked about the 17 topics in 
the TIMSS Assessment Frameworks (Hooper et al., 2013). The answer options were not applicable, very well 
prepared, somewhat prepared, and not well prepared. 
As shown in Exhibit 10.4, teachers’ preparedness to teach the topics varied across the statements. In general, 
across all topics, not less than two thirds of New Zealand Year 5 students were taught mathematics by 
teachers who felt very well prepared to teach the topics. Most Year 5 teachers felt very well prepared to 
teach adding, subtracting, multiplying, and/or dividing with whole numbers (94% of students). Topics like 
comparing and drawing angles (63% of students) and concepts of decimals, including place value and 
ordering, adding and subtracting with decimals (68% of students) had fewer teachers agreeing that they 
were very well prepared to teach them. As shown in Chapter 4, these topics were also less likely to be 
covered in class. 
In general, New Zealand Year 5 teachers were better prepared to teach data display topics than number 
and geometric shapes and measures. New Zealand Year 5 students also performed relatively better on data 
display questions than on questions in the other two areas.
Some of the statements had different wording in TIMSS 2010/11, therefore we cannot directly compare all 
statements. Comparing the statements that had similar wording in both surveys, the proportions of Year 
5 students whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach the topics appears to have increased across 
almost all of them in 2014/15, but the increase was only significant for two topics: using informal coordinate 
systems to locate points in a plane (eight percentage points different) and elementary properties of common 
geometric shapes (seven percentage points different).
Those Year 5 students whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach the topics had significantly higher 
achievement than those whose teachers felt somewhat prepared. There were not enough students whose 
teachers felt not well prepared to report on their achievement level.

Confidence to teach mathematics
Along with asking about preparedness to teach mathematics content, TIMSS also asked teachers to express 
their level of confidence in doing each of the teaching activities shown in Exhibit 10.5. Response options were 
very high, high, medium and low. 
The majority of New Zealand Year 5 teachers reported having high confidence in doing each teaching 
activity. Fewer reported being confident in developing students’ higher-order thinking skills than other 
teaching activities, with 32 percent of Year 5 students having teachers with medium or low confidence in 
this teaching activity. Showing students a variety of problem solving strategies was the activity that most 
teachers were very confident at (with very high and high combined to make 87% of students) followed by 
inspiring students to learn mathematics and making mathematics relevant to students (with very high and 
high combined to make 86% of students on each activity). These statements were asked differently in the 
TIMSS 2010/11 cycle, therefore it is not possible to draw comparison between the two studies.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents

Internationally, New Zealand Year 5 teachers expressed less confidence than many of their peers in other 
countries. Middle primary teachers’ feeling of confidence in doing the listed teaching activities varied a lot 
across countries, and across the type of activity. Japan had the lowest proportion of middle primary students 
with teachers who expressed having very high or high confidence in doing any of the listed activities. 
Generally, countries had the majority of their teachers having high confidence in the teaching activities.

Professional development
TIMSS 2014/15 asked middle primary teachers about the types of professional development they had 
participated in during the past two years. As shown in Exhibit 10.6, the most common type of professional 
development in mathematics teaching for New Zealand Year 5 teachers was around content (74% of 
students) and pedagogy and instruction (70% of students). Mathematics curriculum and addressing 
individual needs of students were also common professional development areas. The least common topic 
was integrating information technology into mathematics, with only 42 percent of Year 5 students whose 
teachers had professional development in this area.
Compared to most of the participating countries, New Zealand teachers more extensively engaged in 
professional development programmes. There were only a few countries (Hong Kong SAR, Singapore, 
Poland, Qatar, UAE and South Africa) like New Zealand where a large proportion of middle primary students 
had teachers who had had professional development in the listed areas. On average internationally, far fewer 
students had teachers who had participated in professional development activities in any of these areas.
The teachers having professional development in these areas did not appear to have a relationship with 
student achievement in general.

development in the past two years in TIMSS 2014/15

Within the professional development, middle primary teachers were also asked to report the amount of 
time they spent in formal in-service professional development in the last two years. As shown in Exhibit 
10.7, slightly more than half of the teachers spent 6 to 35 hours on professional development in the last two 
years. On average internationally, 27 percent of middle primary students had teachers who did not take 
part in any professional development in mathematics in the last two years, while this was only 14 percent 
for New Zealand. Twelve percent of middle primary students had teachers who spent more than 35 hours in 
professional development in the past two years, both in New Zealand and internationally.

Back to Contents
Year 5 mathematics 
teaching and learning 
CHAPTER 11:
 Key Findings
New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about how their teacher engaged with them in their 
mathematics classes, but fewer Year 5 students found their mathematics lessons very engaging 
compared to the international average.
A very high proportion of Year 5 students in New Zealand had teachers report that in every 
or almost every lesson they encouraged their students to express their ideas and encouraged 
classroom discussion among students.
New Zealand Year 5 teachers made less use of whole class teaching compared to other 
countries, and used group activities more often. New Zealand teachers had high use of same 
ability grouping, and relatively high use of having students work on problems (individually or 
with peers) while the teacher was occupied by other tasks. 
Activities where Year 5 teachers explained new mathematics content, teachers explained how 
to solve problems, or teachers asked students to memorise rules, procedures and facts were 
less likely to be used in every or almost every lesson in New Zealand classrooms, compared 
with other countries. 
New Zealand had the highest proportion of Year 5 students with computers available for use 
during mathematics lessons, compared to all other countries.
More Year 5 students had teachers who placed major emphasis on the evaluation of students’ 
ongoing work than on classroom or nationally available tests. On average internationally, many 
more teachers placed major emphasis on classroom tests than in New Zealand.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
95
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
The quality of teaching is a big focus for the Ministry of Education, in partnership 
with the other six key education agencies. Two of the four priorities for education 
for 2016 to 2020 are: "powering up learners, parents, communities and employers 
to influence the quality and relevance of teaching and learning and lift achievement"; 
and "quality teaching, leadership and assessment" (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
According to Anthony & Walshaw (2007), "many students do not succeed with 
mathematics; they are disaffected and continually confront obstacles to engaging 
with the subject." They also point out that quality teaching in mathematics and 
appropriate pedagogical approaches can lead to improved engagement and 
desirable outcomes for learners from diverse social groups.
TIMSS 2014/15 examined the context of student achievement by collecting 
information from students, teachers and school leadership (mainly principals). 
This section will report information collected from teachers and students about 
what happens in classrooms, with a focus on mathematics teaching. Note that this 
section will present information about the mathematics teachers of Year 5 students 
who were usually, but not always, the classroom teacher of the students. In some 
cases, classes were reorganised for mathematics lessons.
Students’ perspectives on teachers’ efforts to engage them 
in mathematics lessons
Year 5 students were asked their agreement to ten statements, shown in Exhibit 11.1, to gauge their level of 
engagement with their mathematics lessons. Their answer options were agree a lot, agree a little, disagree a 
little and disagree a lot. Overall, New Zealand Year 5 students were relatively positive in their responses about 
how engaged they were in their mathematics classes, with no less than 84 percent agreeing (a lot and a little 
combined) to all statements.
The most commonly agreed aspect of mathematics class engagement was teachers doing a variety of 
things to help students to learn, with 94 percent of Year 5 students agreeing to it to some degree, while 
the least agreed upon aspect was teachers letting students show what they had learnt (84%). There was 
an interesting pattern in the mathematics achievement of students across levels of agreement; except my 
teacher does a variety of things to help us learn, for all other nine statements, the Year 5 students who 
agreed a lot to the statement had significantly lower achievement than those who agreed a little. For the 
same statements, the Year 5 students who disagreed a little had significantly higher achievement than those 
who disagreed a lot. For most of the statements, students who agreed a lot had similar achievement to 
those who disagreed a little.

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in Exhibit 11.1 into the Students’ 
views on engaging teaching in mathematics lessons scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values 
on the scale were grouped into three categories: very engaging teaching, engaging teaching, and less than 
engaging teaching. Fewer New Zealand Year 5 students reported experiencing very engaging teaching in 
mathematics lessons than on average internationally (see Exhibit 11.2). The international average shows that 
the more engaged middle primary students were in mathematics lessons, the higher the achievement. 
The pattern for New Zealand Year 5 students, however, differs where students who reported being engaged 
(middle category) had significantly higher achievement than students who were less engaged (16 scale 
score points difference) or those who were very engaged (nine scale score points difference). There was 
no difference in mean achievement for students who were less than engaged and very engaged. This same 
pattern was also observed in the Czech Republic, Sweden, France and the Slovak Republic.

Teachers’ perspectives on instructional techniques
Teachers were asked to report how often they used certain instructional techniques. As Exhibit 11.3 shows, 
86 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were in classes where teachers reported encouraging all students 
to express their ideas, every or almost every lesson. Other common activities included encouraging classroom 
discussion among students (85%), asking students to explain their answers (79%), and linking new content 
to students’ prior knowledge (76%) every or almost every lesson. Less common activities were bringing 
interesting materials to class, completing challenging exercises beyond instruction, and deciding their own 
problem solving problems.

Teachers’ perspectives on learning activities
In addition to instructional techniques, teachers were also asked to report on the extent to which they 
used learning activities in their mathematics lessons (see Exhibit 11.4). Teachers of around half of the Year 
5 students reported that they got their students to work on problems (individually or with peers) with 
teacher guidance, or got them to work in same ability groups, every or almost every lesson (52% and 47% 
respectively). A further 40 percent of Year 5 students (approximately) had teachers report that they got 
their students to do these activities for at least half the lessons (work on problems, individually or with peers, 
with teacher guidance – 40%, or got them to work in same ability groups – 39%). The least common activities 
were having students take a written test or quiz (82% some lessons), memorise rules, procedures and facts 
(66% some lessons), or work in mixed ability groups (53% some lessons).

New Zealand teaching activities markedly differ compared with many other countries. To demonstrate the 
difference between New Zealand and a selection of other countries, Exhibit 11.5 gives the proportions of 
middle primary students whose teachers said they did the listed activities every or almost every lesson. 
New Zealand teachers made less use of whole class teaching compared to other countries, and used group 
activities more often. New Zealand teachers had high use of same ability grouping, and relatively high use of 
having students work on problems (individually or with peers) while the teacher was occupied by other tasks. 
All high performing countries had lower proportions of middle primary students working in same ability 
groups, every or almost every lesson, compared to New Zealand and England. In contrast, high performing 
countries like Japan and the Republic of Korea reported higher proportions of students working in mixed 
ability groups compared to the international average. New Zealand teachers were less likely to use whole 
class teaching frequently (21% every or almost every lesson, compared with 40% on average internationally). 
Just under a third of New Zealand Year 5 students (32%) had teachers who reported they had students work 
on problems (individually or with peers) while the teacher was occupied by other tasks, every or almost every 
day (compared to 16% on average internationally). What these other tasks teachers were occupied with were 
not made clear in this question, nor is the availability of the teachers to help students if necessary. However, 
one interpretation is that teachers were working with one group while another group worked on some other 
assigned activity.
Activities where teachers explained new mathematics content, teachers explained how to solve problems, 
and teachers asked students to memorise rules, facts and procedures were less likely to be used frequently 
in New Zealand classrooms, compared with other countries. Although asking students to memorise was used 
by fewer teachers every lesson in general, New Zealand stands out as having few teachers (8% of students 
in such classes) that use this technique frequently, with only Hong Kong SAR and Finland similar. In contrast, 
memorisation was used every or almost every lesson for around one third of middle primary students in 
Japan (37%), Ireland (34%), and Chinese Taipei (30%). This difference with other countries was also observed 
in TIMSS 2010/11 (12% of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who reported using memorisation, every 
or almost every lesson, in 2010/11). 
It was interesting to observe that 81 percent of middle primary students in Finland had teachers report 
explaining new mathematics content, every or almost every lesson, closely accompanied by Ireland (73% 
of students), compared to 27 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students. For every or almost every lesson, 
almost two-thirds of middle primary students in the Republic of Korea had teachers report that they 
explained how to solve problems (64%), and over half of the students in Japan, the Republic of Korea and 
the United States had teachers report that they did whole class teaching. These figures are significantly 
higher than New Zealand.

Use of resources
Computers
As shown in Exhibit 11.6, New Zealand had the highest proportion of Year 5 students (89%) whose teachers 
responded that computers were available for use during mathematics lessons, compared to all other 
countries. In comparison, 37 percent of students on average internationally were in classes where computers 
were available for use during mathematics lessons. New Zealand also had the highest use of computers to 
explore mathematics principles and concepts, to practice skills and procedures, and to look up ideas and 
information. This is a continued trend from TIMSS 2010/11, where these proportions were the highest in 
New Zealand compared to all participating countries.
In terms of the overall level of accessibility to computers at school, 86 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students 
had teachers report that students had access to shared computers in the classroom. Sixty-two percent of 
students had teachers report that the school had computers the class could use sometimes, and only 10 
percent of students had access to their own computer at school. There was no difference in mean mathematics 
achievement between those that had access to computers and those that did not for all three conditions.

Calculators
Teachers were also asked whether middle primary students in their mathematics classes were permitted to 
use calculators during lessons. As Exhibit 11.7 shows, there were low proportions of students whose teachers 
reported that access to calculators was unrestricted in mathematics lessons. It was interesting to note, 
however, that teachers in three of the highest performing countries, Singapore, Hong Kong SAR and Chinese 
Taipei, reported no access to calculators in lessons for a significant proportion of students. On the other 
hand, New Zealand was very much like Australia, England and Northern Ireland, with teachers reporting that 
a large proportion of their students had restricted access to calculators during mathematics lessons.

Monitoring student progress 
According to the National Administration Guidelines (NAGs), schools in New Zealand are required to gather 
information that is sufficiently comprehensive to enable the progress and achievement of students to be 
evaluated through a range of assessment practices (Ministry of Education, 2015). Teachers were asked how 
much emphasis they placed on different sources to monitor students’ progress in mathematics (sources 
shown in Exhibit 11.8). As shown in the table, more Year 5 teachers placed major emphasis on evaluation of 
students’ ongoing work (84% of students in such classes) than on tests.
On average internationally, proportions of middle primary students were similar to New Zealand for the level 
of major emphasis placed on evaluation of students’ ongoing work and national or regional achievement 
tests. New Zealand practices differed from many other countries in the emphasis placed on classroom tests. 
On average internationally, over two-thirds of middle primary students (68%) had teachers who placed major 
emphasis on this source. Nearly all teachers in the Netherlands (99% of students) and many in Japan (90% of 
students) reported placing major emphasis on classroom tests. Denmark (19%), England (33%), and Sweden 
(32%) were the only countries similar to New Zealand in this aspect. The emphasis on different methods of 
monitoring students’ progress seemed to have no consistent relationship with student achievement.
Not much has changed since TIMSS 2010/11, with most proportions remaining approximately the same, 
except for a small increase in the proportion of students whose teachers have placed little or no emphasis 
on classroom tests (8% in 2010/11).

Back to Contents
Year 5 students’ perceptions 
of climate for learning 
CHAPTER 12:
 Key Findings
On average, nine out of ten New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about their schools, 
teachers and classmates, and two-thirds had a high sense of school belonging. Students who 
had a high sense of belonging had higher mathematics achievement than those with less of a 
sense of belonging.
There were more students in this latest cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that they felt 
safe at school, liked being in school, and felt like they belonged at their school.
New Zealand Year 5 students experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than 
most of the other participating countries. 
Compared to 2010/11, fewer students experienced the individual bullying behaviours: I was 
made fun of or called names; something was stolen from me; and I was hit or hurt (e.g. shoving, 
hitting, kicking) by other student(s). For all other behaviours, the proportion did not change 
significantly between the two cycles.
More boys experienced bullying behaviours than girls. More students experienced bullying 
behaviours in schools with lower socio-economic status. 
Those experiencing bullying behaviours more often had lower mathematics achievement, on 
average, than those experiencing them less often. 

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
105
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
A large body of international and local research indicates a close connection 
between school climate and academic achievement, as well as behavioural and 
social-emotional wellbeing of students (Thapa et al., 2012; O’Malley et al., 2012; 
Ruus et al., 2007; Macneil et al., 2009; Voight et al., 2013; Marsh et al., 2014). 
Students’ perceptions of school climate seems to be positively associated with 
their academic motivation, sense of school belonging, attitudes towards learning 
and positive social behaviours and attitudes in general. Therefore, it is important 
to study the factors that influence how students feel about their school learning 
environment.
TIMSS collects a vast amount of contextual data including students’ responses to 
questions about their school. This chapter examines students’ perceptions of the 
school climate in the context of their sense of school belonging and experience of 
bullying behaviours within the school environment.
Students’ sense of school belonging
To examine the relationship and connection the students have towards their school, teachers and 
classmates, they were asked seven positive agree/disagree statements (see Exhibit 12.1). In addition, 
New Zealand Year 5 students were also asked if they agreed with a further statement I think that students 
at this school care about each other. The response options to all statements were: agree a lot, agree a little, 
disagree a little, and disagree a lot. 
As Exhibit 12.1 shows, most New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about their school, teachers and 
classmates, with almost nine out of every ten students agreeing with all these statements. The statement 
with the highest level of agreement was I like to see my classmates at school with 97 percent of students 
agreeing (a lot and a little combined), followed by I learn a lot at school, with 96 percent agreeing. 
Interestingly though, I think that students at this school care about each other was the least agreed 
statement, with 85 percent of students agreeing with it.

To understand students’ sense of belonging better, the international researchers combined the seven 
statements to create what they termed as the Students’ sense of school belonging scale1. The scale had 
three different categories: high sense of belonging, sense of belonging, and little sense of belonging. 
Sixty-seven percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had a high sense of school belonging, with 29 percent 
having a sense of school belonging, and a small proportion (4%) having little sense of school belonging. 
This was very similar to the international averages, as shown in Exhibit 12.2.
The New Zealand Year 5 students with high sense and sense (the middle category) of school belonging 
had very similar mathematics achievement, on average, both of which were higher than that of students 
who had little sense of belonging (See Exhibit 12.2). This pattern was quite different from the international 
pattern, which was more linear than that of New Zealand Year 5 students.

Students’ sense of school belonging by gender
More New Zealand Year 5 girls had a high sense of school belonging than the Year 5 boys (74% compared 
to 61%), while more boys than girls (6% compared to 2%) had little sense of belonging (see Exhibit 12.3). 
There were no differences in the achievement – girls and boys who had the same sense of school belonging 
had the same achievement scores. New Zealand Year 5 girls and boys had similar patterns of relationship 
between their sense of school belonging and achievement, which followed the pattern of all New Zealand 
Year 5 students, as shown in Exhibit 12.2.

Students’ sense of school belonging by ethnicity
Reflective of all New Zealand Year 5 students, all ethnic groupings had large proportions of students having 
a high sense of school belonging. More Pasifika Year 5 students had a high sense of school belonging than 
non-Pasifika students, while Pakeha/European Year 5 students who had a high sense of belonging were less 
than non-Pakeha/European students. All other ethnic groupings had similar proportion of students who had 
high sense of belonging, with their non-identified ethnic groupings (Exhibit 12.4).
Students of all ethnic groupings who had a high sense of school belonging achieved similar scores to those 
who had just a sense of belonging. Those who had little sense of belonging were too small in number to 
report the achievement results.

Trends in students’ perceptions of school belonging – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
Comparative analysis of the school belonging statements that were asked during the previous TIMSS cycles 
shows that there was an increase in the proportion of middle primary students who agreed with these 
statements in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11 (see Exhibit 12.5). The proportion of middle primary students 
who liked being at school in 2014/15 was similar to that of 2002/2003, and higher than the 2006/2007 and 
2010/2011 results. The statement I think that students at this school care about each other was first asked 
in TIMSS 2006/07. There was no significant change in the proportion of students who agreed with this 
statement since 2006/07.

Students’ experiences of bullying behaviours at school 
A sense of security at school promotes a stable learning environment. Bullying among 
students is a threat to this environment and causes distress to victims, leads to low self-esteem, decreased 
sense of belonging and poor achievements (Hooper et al., 2013). To find out how often they experienced 
any bullying behaviour during the year, Year 5 students were asked eight statements, as shown in Exhibit 
12.6. Their response options were: never, a few times a year, once or twice a month, and at least once a week.
The two most commonly reported bullying behaviours among New Zealand Year 5 students were being 
made fun of or called names and being left out of games or other activities by other students, with similar 
proportions (38% and 37% respectively) of students experiencing each of these behaviours once or twice a 
month or more frequently during the school year. The least commonly reported behaviours were someone 
shared embarrassing information about me and someone threatened me, with one fifth of students 
experiencing it once or twice a month or more frequently in that year.

For all statements, mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students was higher for those who 
experienced bullying behaviours less frequently (ie. responded with a few times a year or never). Some of the 
least commonly experienced behaviours seemed to be making more difference to the students’ achievement 
scores than the most commonly experienced bullying behaviours (Exhibit 12.6).
To describe the extent to which Year 5 students experienced bullying behaviours at school, their responses 
to these statements were combined into the Students bullied at school scale, and the values on the scale 
were grouped into three categories: almost never, about monthly, and about weekly. This data should be read 
with caution, as the scale is base d on individual behaviours and could range from a repeated behaviour on 
a weekly basis from a group of individuals, to four different behaviours, with each behaviour happening on 
separate occasions only once a month and instigated by different perpetrators. However, this still indicates 
the frequency students experienced negative behaviours.

Almost never on the scale meant that the student responded “a few times a year” to four statements at most, and “never” to

New Zealand Year 5 students were more likely to experience bullying behaviours at school than students in 
the majority of TIMSS 2014/15 countries. On the Students bullied at school scale, 60 percent of New Zealand 
Year 5 students experienced bullying behaviour at school about monthly or more often, compared to 44 
percent on average internationally (Exhibit 12.7). 
The New Zealand Year 5 students who almost never experienced bullying behaviours had similar 
achievement as those who experienced it about monthly, while both had higher achievement than those 
who experienced it about weekly. Internationally, most countries followed a consistent pattern where 
middle primary students who experienced bullying more often had lower achievements than those who 
experienced it less often. The Republic of Korea was the only exception, where the level of experiencing 
bullying behaviours at school made no difference to achievement results; Korean middle primary students 
had similar achievement across all categories of the Students bullied at school scale.

Trends in students’ perceptions about school safety – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
The questions given to middle primary students about school safety have changed since TIMSS was first 
implemented in 1994/95. The best comparison can be seen between TIMSS 2014/15 and 2010/11 results, as 
six out of eight statements about students experiencing bullying behaviours at school were asked in both 
cycles, and these statements had the same answer options. 
As Exhibit 12.8 shows, there was a decrease in the proportion of Year 5 students experiencing three out of 
six bullying behaviours once a month or more often in 2014/15 compared to 2010/11 results. There were no 
significant changes to the other statements.

Experiences of bullying behaviours within the different socio-economic 
status schools
The socio-economic status of students is believed to have a strong relationship with their achievement. 
TIMSS 2014/15 asked school principals to report on the economic composition of their school by estimating 
the proportion of students from economically disadvantaged and economically affluent homes. As reported 
in Chapter 7, schools were categorised as being more affluent if more than a quarter of their students were 
from economically affluent homes, and not more than a quarter were from economically disadvantaged 
homes. More disadvantaged schools had the opposite, more than a quarter of their students were from 
economically disadvantaged homes, and not more than a quarter from economically affluent homes. 
All the remaining schools were classified as neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged.
As shown in Exhibit 12.11, more affluent and neither more affluent nor more disadvantaged schools had 
the same proportion of Year 5 students experiencing bullying behaviours. More disadvantaged schools 
however, had a bigger proportion of Year 5 students who experienced bullying behaviours about weekly.

CHAPTER 13:
Year 5 teachers’ 
perceptions of climate 
for learning
 Key Findings
The majority of New Zealand schools (80% of Year 5 students) had high or very high emphasis 
on academic success, according to teachers. Higher school emphasis on academic success was 
associated with higher mathematics scores, both in New Zealand and internationally.
Half of the Year 5 students were taught by teachers who were very satisfied with their job; 96 
percent of teachers were proud of the work they did. 
Seventy-one percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who felt their school was 
very safe and orderly.
Compared with many other countries, New Zealand teachers perceived more challenges in 
their teaching. They wanted to have more time to assist individual students, and preferred to 
have less administrative tasks. 
About two thirds of students studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs were 
very limiting or somewhat limiting their mathematics teaching. These students had lower 
achievement than those who studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs did not 
limit teaching at all.

118
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
Within schools and kura, the quality of teaching has the biggest influence on student 
achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). However, no matter how highly qualified 
teachers can be, without proper resources and support, and positive working 
conditions, this factor alone may not be sufficient in closing the student achievement 
gap. TIMSS 2010/11 results showed higher achievement for schools that provide good 
working conditions for teachers (Hooper et al., 2013). The achievement of important 
academic and social goals is facilitated when the environment is conducive to 
success (Robinson, Hohepa, & Lloyd, 2009). Johnson (2006) and Johnson et al. 
(2012) state that “a manageable workload, adequate facilities,  and the availability 
of instructional materials are important ingredients to fostering productive working 
conditions and promoting teacher satisfaction” (as cited in Hooper et al., 2013). 
Caprara et al. (2006) attests that a positive school culture and environment can 
lead to greater teacher job satisfaction and self-efficacy, which in turn can increase 
student learning (in Hooper et al., 2013). “An orderly environment makes it possible 
for teachers to focus on teaching and students to focus on learning” (Robinson, 
Hohepa, & Lloyd, 2009).
To explore teachers’ perceptions of both the physical and social school 
environments, TIMSS collected data from them on aspects of their schools. 
This included data on teachers’ job satisfaction, teacher interactions, teachers’ 
perceptions of school safety, limitations to mathematics teaching and challenges 
facing teachers. This chapter presents the findings of these analyses.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
119
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
School emphasis on academic success
Middle primary teachers were asked to characterise the different aspects of school life within their school 
that contribute to academic success. The statements varied from teachers, parents and students’ involvement 
in learning and school life, to teacher’s job satisfaction, as listed in Exhibit 13.1. They were given five response 
options: very high, high, medium, low, and very low.
Out of all the 18 statements listed, the aspect of schooling rated highly by the most teachers was teachers’ 
expectations for student achievement, with 94 percent of Year 5 students having teachers who felt that 
teachers had very high or high expectations for student achievement within their school. The more highly 
rated statements were generally about teachers’ characteristics and role in learning. On the least reported 
end of the list, about half of Year 5 students’ teachers thought that parental support for student achievement, 
parental commitment to ensure that students are ready to learn, and parental involvement in school activities 
were not high within their school.
The TIMSS 2014/15 teacher questionnaire included 11 new statements that were not asked in previous cycles, 
but seven out of the 18 statements were included in the TIMSS 2010/11 study as well. Except for teachers’ job 
satisfaction, emphasis on these statements has not changed since 2010/11. The proportion of Year 5 students 
whose teachers felt that the teachers within their school were very highly or highly satisfied with their jobs 
decreased from 81 percent in 2010/11 to 69 percent in 2014/15.

The international researchers combined the responses to 14 out of the 18 statements in the School emphasis 
on academic success scale (see Exhibit 13.2). TIMSS indicators of school emphasis on academic success 
included school administrators’ and teachers’ expectations for successful curriculum implementation 
and student achievement, parental support for student achievement, and the student’s desire to achieve. 
Therefore, the scale didn’t include the statements about teachers’ job satisfaction, clarity of the school’s 
educational objectives, amount of instructional support provided to teachers by school leadership and 
school leadership’s support for teachers’ professional development. To report the scale in a meaningful 
way, values were grouped into three categories; very high, high, and medium emphasis. As the majority of 
responses were very positive, there was no low or very low category in the scale.
As shown in Exhibit 13.2, the majority of New Zealand schools (80%) had high or very high emphasis on 
academic success. Compared to the international average, more New Zealand schools had high or very high 
emphasis on academic success.
Higher school emphasis on academic success reported by the teachers was associated with higher middle 
primary student achievement scores in TIMSS mathematics, both in New Zealand and internationally, even 
though the score difference between the students of very high and high emphasis schools was not significant. 
The mathematics achievement difference between middle primary students whose schools had very high 
emphasis on academic success, and those whose schools had medium emphasis, was higher in New Zealand 
than on average internationally (56 score points in New Zealand, compared with 27 score points on average 
internationally).

Teachers’ job satisfaction
To better understand teachers’ satisfaction with their jobs, they were asked seven statements about their 
feelings of being a teacher, as shown in Exhibit 13.3. The answer options to the question “How often do 
you feel the following way about being a teacher?” were very often, often, sometimes and never or almost 
never. However, due to the very small percentage of never or almost never responses, the graph shows this 
category combined with sometimes as sometimes or less often. 
Overall, New Zealand Year 5 teachers felt very positive about being teachers. Ninety-six percent of Year 5 
students had teachers who were very often or often proud of their work. Most teachers also found their work 
full of meaning and purpose (91% of students very often or often) and were enthusiastic about their job (93% 
of students very often or often). Less than one percent of teachers replied never or almost never to nearly all 
of the statements in the Exhibit 13.3. The statement, I am going to continue teaching for as long as I can was 
the least frequently experienced feeling about being a teacher, with five percent of teachers who never or 
almost never felt this way, and with 79 percent, the lowest among all seven statements, who felt it very often 
or often.

On the basis of responses to these statements, the international researchers created a Teacher job 
satisfaction scale. This scale combines all responses into three categories: very satisfied; satisfied; and less 
than satisfied.
As shown in Exhibit 13.4, half of New Zealand Year 5 students were taught by teachers who were very 
satisfied with their jobs, and 43 percent were taught by teachers who were satisfied. The New Zealand 
proportions were similar to international averages across all levels of job satisfaction. There was not a strong 
relationship between teachers’ job satisfaction and their students’ achievement. Although the graph shows 
small differences between the mean achievement scores of New Zealand Year 5 students depending on their 
teachers’ job satisfaction level, these differences were not significant. Internationally, the pattern differed 
from country to country, but did not have a strong relationship in a lot of countries.

Interactions between teachers
Teachers, as professionals, spend time learning and improving their practice throughout their careers. 
An excellent way to learn is through interactions and collaborations with other teachers. TIMSS asked 
teachers how often they interact with other teachers, with seven types of interaction provided in the 
questionnaire, as shown in Exhibit 13.5. Four possible response options for the frequency of interactions 
were: very often, often, sometimes and never or almost never. 
The first four types of interactions listed in Exhibit 13.5 were equally common among Year 5 teachers, with 
almost three quarters of students having teachers who reported having these interactions very often or 
often. Visiting another classroom to learn more about teaching was the least frequently practiced interaction 
by a considerable margin. The frequency of teacher interactions appeared to have no link with student 
mathematics achievement.

Teachers’ perceptions of school safety and student 
behaviours
To learn what teachers think about their school safety and student behaviour in their schools, teachers of 
Year 5 students were asked to indicate the extent to which they agreed or disagreed with eight statements 
on the general levels of safety they experienced at their schools (see Exhibit 13.6). There were four possible 
response options given: agree a lot, agree a little, disagree a little, and disagree a lot. 
Almost all Year 5 students were taught by teachers who agreed (a lot and a little combined) their school 
was a safe place, with 99 percent agreeing with the statement I feel safe at this school. The least agreed 
statement was the students respect school property, with 13 percent of students having teachers disagree 
to some extent. Five of the eight statements were asked in the TIMSS 2010/11 cycle as well. Comparing the 
responses to these statements in each cycle shows no significant change in the proportions, except for the 
statement the students are well behaved, to which fewer teachers agreed in 2014/15 than in 2010/11 (five 
percentage points fewer).

International researchers summarised teachers’ responses to these statements into a scale called the Safe and 
orderly school scale. This scale describes the extent to which teachers felt their school was a safe and orderly 
environment with three value categories: very safe and orderly, safe and orderly, and less than safe and orderly. 
As shown in Exhibit 13.7, 71 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who agreed that 
their school was very safe and orderly, which was higher than the international average of 56 percent. 
Internationally, middle primary students had higher achievement in schools where the level of safety was 
higher. In New Zealand, Year 5 students who studied in very safe and orderly schools had higher scores than 
those in other category schools (there was no significant difference between these other category schools).

Challenges facing teachers
To find out about the challenges facing Year 5 teachers, they were asked to rate their agreement with 
the statements listed in Exhibit 13.8 using agree a lot, agree a little, disagree a little, and disagree a lot as 
answer options. The most common challenge that the Year 5 teachers were facing was lack of time to assist 
individual students, with 57 percent of students having teachers agreeing a lot, and 35 percent agreeing a 
little to this statement. Having too much material to cover in class and was also amongst the most commonly 
experienced challenges, with 84 percent of teachers agreeing a lot or a little to this statement. The Year 5 
teachers were less likely to report experiencing too much pressure from parents or too many teaching hours, 
compared to other challenges.

The international researchers combined the responses to these statements into a scale called Challenges

many challenges in their teaching, while 31 percent faced few challenges (see Exhibit 13.9). Internationally, 
fewer middle primary teachers were facing many challenges in their teaching. Teachers having few or many 
challenges appeared to have little connection with student mathematics achievement in New Zealand; mean 
achievement scores across all levels of challenges were similar. Internationally, those with few challenges had 
higher scores than those whose teachers were facing many challenges in their teaching.

Limitations to mathematics teaching
In addition to asking about challenges facing teachers, TIMSS asked middle primary teachers to describe to 
what extent the factors listed in Exhibit 13.10 limited mathematics teaching in their classes. Response options 
were: not at all, some, and a lot. 
The most common limitation was students lacking prerequisite knowledge or skills, with 83 percent of Year 
5 students having teachers who thought this factor put some or a lot of limitations on how they taught their 
class. The factors like disruptive students and students suffering from not enough sleep were also largely 
reported as putting a lot or some limitations on mathematics teaching. Students with physical disabilities 
seemed to be the least limiting factor; 88 percent of Year 5 students had teachers who reported that this 
did not limit their teaching at all. However, the research didn’t collect data on what percentage of teachers 
had students with physical disabilities in their class. The fact that not every class has these types of students 
might be an explanation why this wouldn’t be a limiting factor for many teachers.
Exhibit 13.10: Extent to which Year 5 teachers indicated these factors limited their mathematics teaching 
in TIMSS 2014/15

Compared to most TIMSS 2014/15 countries, New Zealand had higher proportions of middle primary students 
whose teachers reported that instruction was limited by students suffering from not enough sleep (72% of 
students) and a lack of basic nutrition (44% of students). The United States was the only English speaking 
country who had more students suffering from not enough sleep and basic nutrition than New Zealand, while 
almost all European and Asian countries had fewer students whose teachers reported these factors limiting 
their teaching.
The international researchers combined the responses to six of the statements into a scale called Teaching 
limited by student needs. The statement students with physical disabilities was not included in the scale. 
The scale had three degrees of limitation: not limited, somewhat limited and very limited.
According to this scale, and similar to the international average, 36 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students 
had teachers who reported having no limitation to their mathematics teaching by students’ needs (see 
Exhibit 13.11). Six percent of students studied in an environment where teachers felt their teaching was very 
limited due to students’ needs.
Limitations to teaching appeared to have strong relationship with student mathematics achievement; the 
more teaching was limited by students’ needs, the lower the achievement. A similar pattern was observed for 
many other countries, as well as shown by the international average.

Class size
TIMSS asked teachers about the size of their classes, as larger or smaller classes can influence how the 
teacher chooses to teach mathematics topics. At Year 5 level, composite classes combining different year 
levels are a common feature in New Zealand primary schools.1 The average TIMSS class size for mathematics 
in New Zealand was 27 students in 2014/15, out of which 16 students were at the Year 5 level. The 
international average class size was 25 students. In the majority of countries, average class size ranged from 
20 to 30 students; Chile (32), Morocco (32), Singapore (35), Slovak Republic (19) and Cyprus (19) were the 
few exceptions. 
It is difficult to draw a conclusion about the relationship between class size and achievement. For example, in 
some countries smaller classes tend to be in rural areas where there are fewer resources, and larger classes 
in urban areas with more resources. Remedial classes may also be smaller. However, TIMSS shows that high 
performing Asian countries have relatively larger class sizes (ranging from 25 to 35 in TIMSS 2014/15), while 
most non-Asian top performing countries tend to have smaller class sizes (between 20 and 27 students in 
TIMSS 2014/15). The New Zealand Year 5 class size has varied over the different TIMSS cycles. It consistently 
decreased from 29 students in 1994/95, to 25 students in 2010/11, and increased to 27 students in 2014/15.
1	
New Zealand is unusual in the prevalence of composite classes. TIMSS 2014/15 data showed only Belgium and Australia in the 46 
country sample had at least 50% composite classes at the middle primary level.
