Back to Contents
Year 5 principals’ perceptions 
of climate for learning 
CHAPTER 14:
 Key Findings
The principals were very positive about their teachers, with more than 80 percent of Year 5 
students having principals who rated the teachers’ role in the school’s academic success as 
very high or high. Principals were less positive about parents’ support and involvement than 
about their teachers and students. 
Principals’ perceptions of their school’s academic climate doesn’t appear to have changed 
much since 2002/03. The only big change is observed on parental support for student 
achievement, with a significant drop from 72 percent in 2010/11 to 56 percent in 2014/15.
The principals and teachers had similar views on most aspects of the school’s academic 
success, the only difference of opinion was observed on the students’ respect for classmates 
who excel in school, about which the principals were more positive than the teachers.
More New Zealand schools reported high or very high emphasis on academic success, 
compared to the international average and to most English-speaking and Asian high-achieving 
countries. Students studying in schools with higher emphasis on academic success had higher 
mathematics achievement scores. 
Compared to other countries, New Zealand principals reported fewer problems with school 
discipline. Over two-thirds of Year 5 students in New Zealand (71%) attended schools where 
the principal reported hardly any problems with school discipline. Being in a school with less 
of a problem was associated with higher achievement in mathematics, on average. There has 
been no change in the level of discipline in New Zealand schools since TIMSS 2010/11.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
131
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
Principals may not be part of classroom teaching, but they have a strong indirect 
influence on education achievement. Hitt and Tucker (2015) established five 
essential broad domains of effective leader practices that positively align with 
student achievement: establishing and conveying the vision; facilitating a high-
quality learning experience for students; building professional capacity; creating 
an effective and supportive environment for learning; and connecting with external 
partners. As part of the Investing in Educational Success programme, the Ministry 
of Education introduced a Principal Recruitment Allowance to help New Zealand’s 
most high-need schools and kura attract highly-effective principals who can provide 
the leadership needed to lift student achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
To be able to effectively lead, principals need to see the “bigger picture”, and to 
have knowledge and insight into the complex processes taking place in their school 
(Ministry of Education, 2008). 
TIMSS collected data on principals’ perceptions of their school environment, in 
conjunction with other factors that contribute to effective schools. This chapter will 
present findings on principals’ perceptions of their school in relation to the students’ 
achievement. 
Principals’ perceptions of climate for learning
Principals of Year 5 students were asked how they would characterise thirteen aspects of life at their school, 
listed in Exhibit 14.1. The response options were: very high, high, medium, low, and very low.
The statements the principals were most positive about were mainly related to teachers, with more than 80 
percent of students having principals who indicated these aspects were very high or high. The majority of 
principals indicated that teachers’ expectations for student achievement (90% of students) and teachers’ 
understanding of the school’s curricular goals (89% of students) at their school were very high or high. Other 
statements about teachers had slightly lower proportions, with the lowest being teachers’ ability to inspire 
students (84%).
Principals were also very positive about students’ respect for classmates who excel at school, with 88 
percent of students having principals who indicated this was very high or high. However, they were less 
positive about other student related aspects like students’ desire to do well at school (80% of students) and 
students’ ability to reach school’s academic goals (75% of students). 
Compared to their responses about teachers and students, responses by the principals to parental support and 
involvement were less positive. Parental commitment to ensure that students are ready to learn, the lowest 
of the five statements, had only about half of Year 5 students whose school principals indicated this aspect 
was very high or high. Of the five statements, more principals were positive about parental expectations for 
student achievement, with 71 percent of students having principals who indicated this was very high or high.

Year 5 teachers were asked the same questions as principals and responded similarly (see Exhibit 14.2). 
While percentages differed slightly across all statements, a significant difference was only observed for 
one statement, students’ respect for classmates who excel in school, about which the principals were more 
positive than teachers.
The responses of the principals across these thirteen statements, and their relationship with mathematics 
achievement, differed depending on the statements. The general pattern observed was, the higher the 
characterisation of the aspect, the higher the mathematics achievement. The parental support and 
involvement statements, and the statements about students, followed this general pattern. However, the 
principals’ responses to the statements about teachers didn’t appear to have a significant relationship 
with student achievement, except for teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum. 
The Year 5 students in schools where principals responded very high or high to this statement had higher 
achievement than those where principals responded medium.

The international researchers combined the principals’ responses to these statements in the School emphasis 
on academic success scale (see Exhibit 14.3). However, this scale is different from the same named scale 
created on the basis of teachers’ responses (see Exhibit 13.2), as the latter included one extra statement, 
collaboration between school leadership and teachers to plan instruction, which was not asked to the 
principals. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories, very high, high, 
and medium emphasis. As principals had responded very positively to these statements, there was no low or 
very low category on this scale. 
As shown in Exhibit 14.3, the majority of New Zealand schools (79%) had high or very high emphasis on 
academic success. Compared to the international average, more New Zealand schools had high and very 
high emphasis on academic success. Thirteen out of 49 TIMSS 2014/15 countries, including high achievers 
like Norway, Belgium (Flemish), Finland and the Netherlands, had one percent or less of their middle primary 
students studying in schools with very high emphasis on academic success, with the majority of students 
being in schools with medium emphasis on academic success. New Zealand had more schools in the 
very high emphasis on academic success category than most English speaking and Asian high-achieving 
countries. 
The general pattern, as observed both by the international average and in New Zealand, was that the 
higher on the scale the emphasis by the principal, the higher the associated achievement scores in TIMSS 
for mathematics. In New Zealand, there was a difference of 62 achievement score points for mathematics, 
on average, between those Year 5 students whose principals rated very high on the scale, and those who 
rated medium, while it was 37 score points on the international average. About one third of the TIMSS 
2014/15 countries had less than 20 score points difference in student achievement between higher and lower 
emphasis categories.

Trends in principals’ perceptions of school climate and 
academic success
The questions given to principals about school climate were first introduced in TIMSS 2002/03. Comparison 
of the statements included since 2002/03 shows little change across the cycles on most of the statements. 
As shown in Exhibit 14.4, compared to TIMSS 2010/11 results, in 2014/15 fewer principals rated students’ desire 
to do well in school (significant drop from 88 to 80% in student proportion) and parental support for student 
achievement (significant drop from 72 to 56% in student proportion) in their school as being very high or high.

Principals’ perceptions of school discipline and student 
behaviours
As school leader, the principal plays a major role in building and sustaining an effective learning environment 
and a positive school climate. TIMSS 2014/15 asked the school principals how much of a problem a series of 
behaviours were in their school among Year 5 students (see Exhibit 14.5). Principals expressed the extent to 
which these behaviours were a problem with one of the following response options: not a problem, minor 
problem, moderate problem, or serious problem. The majority of Year 5 students attended schools where 
these behaviours were perceived by the principal to be a minor problem at the most. Few students had 
principals that acknowledged any of these behaviours as posing a serious problem in their school. The most 
common behaviours causing moderate and serious problems of school discipline were arriving late at school, 
absenteeism and classroom disturbance, all with less than 10 percent of Year 5 students.

To summarise the extent to which school discipline affects student learning, the TIMSS international team 
created the School discipline problems scale, based on principals’ views on the extent to which the ten 
behaviours listed above occurred among middle primary students in their schools. To report the scale in a 
meaningful way, values on the scale were grouped into three categories; moderate to severe problems, minor 
problems, and hardly any problems.
Over two-thirds of Year 5 students in New Zealand (71%) attended schools whose principals indicated 
that there were hardly any problems with school discipline, and only one percent attended schools where 
there were moderate to severe problems. New Zealand principals were more positive than on average 
internationally (60 % hardly any problems and 10% moderate to severe problems). All of the English-
speaking countries who participated in TIMSS 2014/15 had a proportion of students similar to or above the 
international average in the hardly any problems category, as did a number of the higher-achieving countries. 
This finding appears to contrast with student reports of bullying behaviours at school; New Zealand was one 
of the countries with the most Year 5 students regularly experiencing bullying behaviours (see Exhibit 12.7).

Overall, being in a school with less of a problem was associated with higher achievement in mathematics, 
both in New Zealand and on average internationally. However, this was not consistent over all countries; 
several countries showed no significant variation in achievement scores across the categories. Japan and 
Chinese Taipei were among these countries. In some countries, such as Georgia, students whose school was 
categorised as having moderate to severe problems had higher average achievement scores than those in 
the minor problems category, and similar to those in the hardly any problems category . For some other 
countries, like Slovenia for example, there was a higher score for those with moderate to severe problems 
over either of the other categories.
Trends in principals’ perceptions of school discipline
Even though the statements about school discipline were asked in previous TIMSS cycles as well, in 2002/03 
and 2006/07 this question was about the frequency of a series of problem behaviours occurring in the 
schools, and how severe a problem these behaviours were. In 2010/11, the question was adjusted to only ask 
how much of a problem these behaviours were in the school, with different answer options compared to the 
previous two cycles. Due to these differences, we are able to compare the results across only TIMSS 2010/11 
and 2014/15. The comparison of the individual statements and the School discipline problems scale results 
shows no change in the level of discipline in New Zealand schools since TIMSS 2010/11.

Year 5 parents’ perceptions 
of climate for learning 
CHAPTER 15:
 Key Findings
In general, parents had very positive perceptions of their child’s school. Students whose 
parents were less than satisfied with their child’s school had lower mathematics achievement 
than those who were more satisfied.
Nearly all parents (97%) agreed that their child’s school provided a safe environment for 
learning. Students in these schools had a lot higher achievement than those whose parents 
thought their school didn’t provide a safe environment.
A higher percentage of parents felt that schools were doing a good job in helping the students 
to become better in mathematics or reading than in science. Compared to 2010/11, in 2014/15 
more parents agreed that their child’s school did a good job in helping him/her become better 
in science.

140
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
The four year plan of the Ministry of Education highlights that educational success 
depends on the efforts and support of not just schools, teachers and principals, but 
also parents, whānau and communities. The action plan places important focus on 
working more closely with parents, family and whānau, communities and employers 
to raise student achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
Research shows that, one of the attributes of a well-functioning school is having a 
mutually trustful relationship with parents, and their active involvement in school 
life and learning. Schools that encourage and welcome parental involvement are 
more likely to have highly involved parents than schools that do not make an effort 
to keep parents informed and participating (Jeynes, 2005, in Hooper et al., 2013). 
Students whose parents are actively involved in their school tend to have a better 
academic performance, fewer behavioural problems and overall a positive attitude 
toward school (Henderson & Berla, 1994; Taylor et al., 2000; Jeynes, 2005; Dearing 
et al., 2006; Jeynes, 2007; Hill & Tyson, 2009).
To better understand the effects of the home, TIMSS 2014/15 collected a range 
of data from student’s parents or caregivers. Parents’ perceptions of climate for 
learning and performance are discussed in this chapter.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Parents’ perceptions of school performance
Parents of students who participated in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to describe how much they agreed or 
disagreed with statements about the different aspects of the school their child was attending, as listed in 
Exhibit 15.1. The response options were agree a lot, agree a little, disagree a little, and disagree a lot. In New 
Zealand, 56 percent of parents returned completed questionnaires, so the results in this section should be 
read with this in mind. 
Overall, parents who responded to the questionnaire were very positive about their child’s school. As 
shown in Exhibit 15.1, over 90 percent of parents agreed with five out of eight statements. Somewhat lower 
percentages of parents agreed that their child’s school does a good job informing them of his/her progress 
(87%), and promotes high academic expectations (86%). Parents’ perceptions of school safety were very 
positive, with 97 percent of students’ parents agreeing that their child’s school provides a safe environment. 
A higher percentage of parents agreed that the school was doing a good job in helping their child become 
better in mathematics or reading (90% and 92% respectively) than helping their child become better in 
science (81%). A similar pattern was observed in TIMSS 2010/11, which may reflect that there is still strong 
focus on literacy and numeracy in schools. However, compared to 2010/11, more parents agreed that their 
child’s school does a good job in helping him/her become better in science in 2014/15. The results remained 
unchanged for all other statements.

The international researchers combined the responses to these statements in a scale called Parents’ 
perception of school performance, which had three categories; very satisfied, satisfied and less than satisfied 
(see the Exhibit 15.2). 
Sixty-three percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had parents who were very satisfied with the school 
their child was attending, and only six percent were less than satisfied. Compared to the international 
average, New Zealand had slightly more students whose parents were very satisfied about their child’s school 
performance (63% compared to 59% on international average). However, there were some big differences 
across the TIMSS countries; proportions on the very satisfied category ranged from seven percent (Japan) 
to 91 percent of middle primary students (Kazakhstan). Australia, the Netherlands, Norway, England and 
the United States had data available for less than 50 percent of students, and were therefore not included 
in the analysis.

about 40 percent of the countries. In a few countries, like the Czech Republic, Slovak Republic and Serbia, 
students whose parents were very satisfied with their school had lower or similar achievement to those in 
other categories.

School resources for 
Year 5 students
CHAPTER 16:
 Key Findings
Teachers most commonly reported lack of technological resources, lack of support for using 
technology, and inadequate workspace for teachers as moderate or serious problems at their 
school. New Zealand Year 5 teachers reported fewer problems, in general, than many of their 
international counterparts.
Principals most commonly reported a lack of technologically competent staff hindered 
instruction at their school. Many principals also felt that a lack of teachers with a specialisation 
in mathematics hindered mathematics instruction. Other resources commonly reported as 
hindering instruction included computer software and library resources for mathematics 
instruction, computers for teaching and learning, and resources for students with disabilities. 
The less affected the principal felt instruction at the school was by a shortage of mathematics 
resources, the higher the mathematics achievement scores.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
145
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
The quality and nature of the physical environment of schools and resources have 
been thought to have a significant influence on teacher attitudes, behaviours and 
performance, which have been directly linked to student outcomes and achievement 
(Dawson & Parker, 1998; Schneider, 2002; Buckley, Schneider, & Shang, 2004). Uline 
and Tschannen-Moran (2008) found a statistically significant relationship between 
the quality of school facilities, school climate and achievement in mathematics.
In this chapter, we explore the physical environment of our schools and resources 
to gain an understanding of how the teaching environment and resource shortages 
potentially impact teaching and mathematics achievement in TIMSS. Information on 
these has been obtained from the contextual questionnaires filled in by teachers and 
principals in participating schools.
Quality and availability of school buildings and resources, 
according to teachers
Teachers in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to describe the extent to which various issues created problems 
for them, as listed in Exhibit 16.1. They were given four response options: not a problem; minor problem; 
moderate problem; and serious problem.
Over half of Year 5 students had teachers who identified inadequate workspaces, inadequate technological 
resources and support for using technology as the issues they faced in their school (minor, moderate, or 
serious problem). The least problematic of these issues, in the opinions of the teachers were the cleanliness 
of their classrooms (not a problem for 78% of students), maintenance work on classrooms (60% not a 
problem) and not having adequate instructional materials and supplies (60% not a problem).

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in the Problems with school 
conditions and resources scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values on the scale were grouped 
into three categories: moderate to severe problems; minor problems; and hardly any problems. 
As Exhibit 16.2 shows, New Zealand teachers were relatively positive about their working conditions, with a 
small proportion reporting moderate to severe problems with school conditions and resources, compared 
with teachers from other countries. Although New Zealand teachers were not as positive as teachers from 
England, Australia, Hong Kong SAR and Singapore, fewer New Zealand teachers were negative about their 
working conditions compared with their counterparts in some high-achieving countries. 
According to the international average, the more positive teachers were on the scale (that is the less likely they 
were to report on any problem), the higher the average mathematics achievement of middle primary students 
(see Exhibit 16.2). However, in New Zealand there was no significant difference in mathematics achievement 
between those who reported moderate to severe problems, minor problems or hardly any problems.

Impact of shortages of resources, according to principals
Principals were asked to rate if their school’s capacity to provide instruction was affected by a shortage 
or inadequacy of general and mathematics resources, as listed in Exhibit 16.3. This question was asked 
differently from the question asked of teachers, with the options being: a lot; some; a little; or not at all. 
Although some principals found the lack of particular mathematical resources hindered instruction, its 
perceived impact was smaller compared to the lack of certain science related resources. This pattern was 
also observed in 2010/11 (see Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013b; Caygill, Singh, & Hanlar, 2016). Of the 
resources listed, the ones most commonly seen as having an impact on mathematics instruction (a lot, some 
and a little combined) were a lack of teachers with a specialisation in mathematics, computer software, and 
library resources for mathematics instruction. As shown in chapter 10, fewer New Zealand Year 5 teachers 
had a specialisation in mathematics when compared with many other countries. Of the general resources, a 
lack of technologically competent staff, resources for students with disabilities, and computers for teaching 
and learning were the most common resources indicated as hindering instruction. Although there were a few 
changes to this question since TIMSS 2010/11, the lack of technologically competent staff and computers for 
instruction were also most commonly reported as issues in that cycle.

Compared to 2006/07, in 2010/11 more Year 5 students were in schools where lack of resources limited 
instructional capability. In TIMSS 2014/15, compared with 2010/11, this trend was partially reversed; there was 
an increase in the proportion of principals responding not at all to most resources, except for instructional 
spaces, school buildings and grounds, and a lack of technologically competent staff. There was little change 
in the proportions responding a lot.

The international researchers combined principals’ responses to the statements in Exhibit 16.3 into the 
Instruction affected by mathematics resource shortages scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, 
values on the scale were grouped into three categories: not affected; affected; and affected a lot. As Exhibit 
16.4 shows, 37 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were in schools where principals had indicated that 
resource shortages had not affected mathematics instruction. Sixty-two percent of Year 5 students were in 
schools where instruction was somewhat affected by shortages in mathematics resources, and one percent 
of students were affected a lot. These proportions are not significantly different from 2010/11 (see Caygill, 
Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013b).

In comparison, fewer middle primary students (27%) were in schools internationally, on average, where 
principals felt that instruction was not at all affected by mathematics resource shortages. Principals in the 
Republic of Korea reported as high as 73 percent of students not being affected by resource shortages. 
On the other hand, Hong Kong SAR and Japan had a smaller proportion of middle primary students, 
compared to New Zealand, where principals felt that instruction was not affected by mathematics resource 
shortages. In contrast, Singapore, a high performing country, had at least eight percent of students that were 
affected a lot by mathematics resource shortages. 
The international average shows that, in general, the less affected the school was by a shortage of 
mathematics resources, the higher the achievement scores (see Exhibit 16.4). This general finding held true 
for most countries, including New Zealand, but the Republic of Korea and Japan were exceptions. This 
implies that, in general, resourcing is important in ensuring good achievement outcomes. However, as is 
indicated by principals, the teachers need to know how to use the resources, and to use them appropriately, 
to ensure they have a positive impact on the student outcomes.

Student abilities at 
school entry
CHAPTER 17:
 Key Findings
Students who had attended an early childhood facility for more than one year had higher 
mathematics achievement by Year 5 than those who had attended for less time, or not at all.
Parents’ reports show that many students begin school already having some numeracy 
abilities, and that this early preparation has a positive effect on their achievement later on.
According to principals’ estimates of the numeracy abilities of students when they began 
school, mathematics achievement at Year 5 was higher in schools where the cohort were more 
mathematically able when they were new entrants. 
In general, more affluent schools were more likely to report higher proportions of able students 
in their school intake.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
151
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
Pre-primary education (or early childhood education, ECE) builds on early learning 
children may receive at home, and helps prepare them for a future of learning, 
including primary school. As mentioned in Chapter 8, home circumstances influence 
the achievement of school-age children. In addition, the benefits of quality early 
childhood education have long been acknowledged in numerous publications. 
There is a wide range of early childhood education services available to children in 
New Zealand, and government subsidies enable all children to have access to them. 
Although not compulsory in New Zealand, the Government aims to increase 
participation in ECE (Ministry of Education, 2012). Partnering Up was established 
in 2010 with the aims of raising overall participation in ECE. The programme has 
been hailed a success, reporting in December 2015 that 96 percent of children had 
participated in ECE prior to starting school. In particular, more Māori and Pasifika 
children attended ECE in 2015 (in December, 94% and 92% respectively) than in 2010 
(in June, 90% and 86% respectively – Ministry of Education, 2016). 
Early childhood education in New Zealand takes place in a mixture of teacher-led 
and parent-led services. The early childhood curriculum framework, Te Whāriki, is 
used by most New Zealand ECE services to guide children’s learning opportunities. 
The framework is made up of five strands: well-being, belonging, contribution, 
communication, and exploration; and the curriculum specifies particular learning 
outcomes associated with the goals of each strand. Learning outcomes most relevant 
to the numeracy skills surveyed in TIMSS are found in the communication strand, and 
include familiarity with numbers and their uses, as well as skills in using the counting 
system, mathematical symbols and concepts (Ministry of Education, 1996). 
This chapter will examine early childhood education in the New Zealand context, 
along with principals’ and parents’ responses when asked to estimate the abilities of 
children when they first began school.

152
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»

Early childhood education in New Zealand
The Early learning survey administered to parents or caregivers of Year 5 students in TIMSS 2014/15 
requested information on the length of time spent in ECE prior to starting school, the types of ECE 
establishments attended the year prior to starting school, and the hours spent at these each week. 
Note that only 56 percent of New Zealand parents returned the questionnaire, so values refer to only these 
students and their parents. As shown in Exhibit 17.1, over half of Year 5 students (56%) had participated 
in early childhood education as pre-schoolers for three years or more. The students with three years or 
more in ECE had a significantly higher mean mathematics achievement score (518) than the relatively small 
proportion (5%) of Year 5 students who had not participated at all (478). Higher mathematics achievement 
scores were associated with more time spent in ECE for both New Zealand Year 5 students, and on average 
internationally. This finding suggests that some early childhood education is better than none or very little.

In most TIMSS countries, pre-primary education or early childhood education (ECE) is voluntary, although 
participation rates are high. In other TIMSS countries, one year of pre-primary education is compulsory (e.g. 
Denmark and Hungary). The length of participation in ECE varies across countries, which may be influenced 
by their various school starting ages. This means that the context for early childhood education within each 
country needs to be considered before making cross-country comparisons. For example, countries like 
Denmark have a school starting age of 7, so are more likely to report higher proportions of children attending 
ECE for three years or more compared to Northern Ireland, where the majority of children start primary 
school at the age of 4. Therefore, it is probably more beneficial to look at how ECE participation is related to 
achievement within a country (Chamberlain, 2012).

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
153
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
Comparisons between the 2010/11 and 2014/15 TIMSS cycles on the changes in length of ECE participation 
cannot be made as the categories used in each cycle differ greatly. It is worth noting, however, that both 
cycles observed a general trend where more time spent in ECE before primary school was associated with 
higher mathematics achievement. 
The number of hours in early childhood education prior to starting school
New Zealand Year 5 students participating in TIMSS 2014/15 probably started school sometime over the 
period June 2010 through to May 2011, and would have been eligible for 20 Hours ECE, launched in July 2007. 
This policy enables all three-, four- and five-year olds to receive up to 20 hours a week of free early childhood 
education in all teacher-led services and some kōhanga reo (Ministry of Education, 2016). Children from low-
income families are also eligible for extra subsidies (Ministry of Education, 2016). As mentioned previously, 
parents or caregivers of Year 5 students in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to estimate the number of hours their 
child had attended an early childhood education facility in the year prior to starting school. Of those whose 
parents/caregivers responded, almost two-thirds of Year 5 students had spent more than 20 hours per week 
in ECE in the year immediately before they had started school. More hours spent each week in ECE services 
were associated with higher mathematics scores, on average (see Exhibit 17.2).

There are various types of ECE services available in New Zealand, and many children attend more than 
one type of service. Exhibit 17.3 shows a breakdown of the types of ECE services Year 5 students attended 
the year immediately before starting school. Of the total number of students whose parents or caregivers 
indicated they had attended ECE, around 36 percent of those students had attended more than one ECE 
service in the year before starting school. It is important to note that playgroups, although recognised as a 
form of ECE in New Zealand, are generally not viewed as ECE in the international context.

Estimate of abilities at school entry 
Parents’ reports
Parents or caregivers were asked to estimate their child’s early literacy and numeracy skills when they first 
began primary school. When reading this section, remember that only 56 percent of parents or caregivers 
returned questionnaires. Parents or caregivers of New Zealand students were asked whether their child 
could: count by himself/herself, recognise written numbers, write numbers, do simple addition, do simple 
subtraction, count money, and measure lengths or heights. The results of these questions were summarised 
into a measure of how well children could do numeracy tasks when they began school. As shown in Exhibit 
17.4, over half of New Zealand Year 5 parents thought their children could do the early numeracy tasks 
moderately well or very well (54%). Almost half of parents or caregivers who responded thought that their 
children could not do these tasks well (46%).

Although not directly relevant to early mathematics, parents or caregivers were also asked about students’ 
literacy skills upon beginning school. These tasks were: recognise most of the letters of the alphabet, read 
some words, read sentences, read a story, write letters of the alphabet, and write some words. The results

proportion of students thought not able to do the early literacy tasks well was the same as for the numerical 
tasks (46% not well for both). 
As Exhibit 17.4 and Exhibit 17.5 show, students that were deemed able to do early numeracy and literacy tasks 
very well before starting school had significantly higher mean mathematics achievement scores compared 
to Year 5 students who could do these tasks moderately well or not well at all when they started school. 
Parents’ or caregivers’ reports on whether students could perform early numeracy and literacy tasks when 
they began primary school illustrates that the advantage of early abilities appears to still be evident in Year 5.

Principals’ reports
Principals were asked to estimate what proportions of students in their school could do a range of early 
literacy and numeracy skills when they began primary school. Numeracy skills were based on the following 
statements: count up to 100 or higher, recognise written numbers from 1-10, recognise written numbers 
higher than 10, write numbers from 1-10, do simple addition, and do simple subtraction. These statements are 
similar, but not the same, to those asked of the parents. As Exhibit 17.6 shows, the most common numeracy 
skill for new entrants was the ability to recognise written numbers from 1 to 10. Seventy-two percent of 
students attended schools where at least a quarter of the students could recognise written numbers 
from 1 to 10 by the time they started school. Fewer new entrants could do the other tasks; a majority of 
New Zealand Year 5 students were in schools where less than a quarter of their peers could count up to 
100, recognise written numbers higher than 10, write all ten numbers from 1 to 10 or do simple addition or 
subtraction prior to school entry. 
As Exhibit 17.6 shows, higher mathematics achievement is observed for schools where more than half of the 
students have these specific numerical skills, compared to schools where fewer students have those specific 
numerical skills when they start their first year of school.

Principals were also asked about students’ literacy skills upon beginning school, with regards to their ability 
to: recognise most letters of the alphabet, read some words, read sentences, write letters of the alphabet, 
write some words; construct sentences orally; and retell and sequence story events. The most common 
literacy skill for new entrants was the ability to construct sentences orally. The next most common skills were 
recognising most letters of the alphabet and writing letters of the alphabet. The least common skills were 
the reading abilities and writing words. Like numerical skills, higher mathematics achievement was observed 
for schools where more than half of the students had these literacy skills, compared to schools where fewer 
students began school with these specific literacy skills.  
Responses to literacy and numeracy abilities were aggregated to produce an indicator of school readiness. 
As mentioned previously, there is substantial variation in the average age of entry into primary schools and 
prior educational experiences across countries, which need to be considered when making cross-country 
comparisons. New Zealand students typically begin primary school on, or soon after, their fifth birthday. 
The average age of school entry is 6 years or older for Singapore, the Republic of Korea, Hong Kong SAR, 
Chinese Taipei and Japan, around 5 years for Australia, and 4 years for Northern Ireland. It is interesting to 
note that high performing countries which have the same school starting age shared similar proportions of 
literacy and numeracy skills amongst students entering schools. Similarly, Australia and New Zealand, where 
students start school at the age of 5, shared similar proportions (see Exhibit 17.7). Within New Zealand, 
schools where less than a quarter of students were estimated to have these early literacy and numeracy skills 
at school entry had lower mathematics achievement, on average, than those where a higher proportion of 
students started school with these skills.

Early numeracy and literacy abilities and school socio-
economic composition
Schools that were more economically disadvantaged were more likely to have a low proportion of their 
students having numeracy and literacy skills at school entry than schools from more affluent communities 
(see Exhibit 17.8). For more information on this categorisation, see Chapter 7. Among the more disadvantaged 
schools in New Zealand, the majority of principals (82% of students in such schools) reported that less than 
25 percent of students who entered their schools had early numeracy and literacy abilities. No schools in this 
category reported that 75 percent or more of their students entered their school with these skills. 
Among the more affluent schools, only 33 percent of principals reported that less than 25 percent of their 
students entered their school with these skills, and 11 percent of principals reported that more than 75 
percent of their students entered their school with these skills.

These findings appear to reinforce the view that the numeracy and literacy skills that children begin school

importance of placing emphasis on early childhood education.

Conclusion
Mathematics achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
The average (mean) mathematics achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students in 2014/15 was higher than 
that of the students 20 years ago, but is not significantly different from 2002/03, 2006/07, and 2010/11. 
Although New Zealand’s mean in mathematics has increased since 1994/95, many other countries have 
increased by more. New Zealand Year 5 students’ mean mathematics achievement was significantly higher 
than 13 countries, but lower than the mean score of 33 countries, including all the other predominantly 
English-speaking countries who participated. 
Was our average achievement low compared with other countries ­because the test was inappropriate for 
New Zealand students? When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for Year 5 students, 
there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when the test was reduced to 
contain only those questions appropriate to New Zealand Year 5 students, the average student got less than 
half the items correct. The appropriateness of the questions was judged against the National Standards, which 
at the Year 5 level states that by the end of their fifth year of schooling, students will be achieving at early level 
3 in the mathematics and statistics learning area of the curriculum (Ministry of Education, 2009). According 
to their teachers, many Year 5 students were working at level 2 of the curriculum by the end of the year, rather 
than level 3. Perhaps not surprisingly, those students working at higher levels had higher achievement.
Strengths and weaknesses in Year 5 mathematics 
achievement 
New Zealand Year 5 students consistently, since 2006/07, have shown greatest strength at statistics 
questions (called data display in TIMSS) compared to number and geometry and measurement. Data 
display was also the area of the curriculum most likely to be covered by their teachers in the lessons. New 
Zealand teachers also felt better prepared to teach data display topics than number and geometric shapes 
and measures. This is a similar finding to other studies such as the Programme for International Student 
Assessment (PISA), which also finds that New Zealand students perform best, on average, on tasks related to 
uncertainty and data (May, 2013).
New Zealand Year 5 students did significantly better at applying their knowledge and reasoning compared to 
the cognitive behaviour of knowing. This has been a consistent pattern since 2006/07. Most Year 5 students 
had teachers who expressed high or very high confidence in their own ability to teach mathematics. Of the 
statements listed, the most confidence was expressed in showing students a variety of problem solving 
strategies and the least confidence was expressed in developing students’ higher-order thinking skills.
Equity among Year 5 students
“Over many years, the New Zealand education system has been characterised by relatively high levels of 
inequality” (Ministry of Education, 2016). One way to examine equity is to look at the gap between the 
high and low performers. The gap (range) for Year 5 students was wider in 2014/15 than in 2010/11, but not 
as wide as 1994/95. Six percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were classified as advanced performers 
(reached the advanced benchmark), while 16 percent were classified as below low performers, who did not 
perform simple mathematics tasks. Compared with other countries, New Zealand was around the middle 
when considering the proportion of advanced performers, but had a high proportion of lower performers. 
High performing countries had more than one-third of their students classified as advanced performers, and 
one percent or fewer classified as below low performers.
The proportions of Year 5 students in each classification in 2014/15 were not significantly different from 
2002/03 (the TIMSS cycle with the best overall results). The proportion of high-achieving students, those 
classified as high or advanced performers, has increased significantly between 2010/11 and 2014/15.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
161
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
In general, the difference between girls and boys in mathematics has not been marked in recent years. 
Year 5 boys and girls in 2014/15 had the same achievement, on average, but boys had a wider range and 
more advanced achievers than girls. 
Equity among ethnic groupings remains a concern. Nearly one-quarter of all Year 5 students identified 
themselves as belonging in more than one ethnic grouping; the groupings were Pākehā/European, 
Māori, Pasifika, Asian, and Other. There was a wide variation in mathematics achievement within ethnic 
groupings, with advanced achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings. Māori had lower average 
mathematics achievement than non-Māori. Similarly, Pasifika students had lower average mathematics 
achievement than non-Pasifika. There was evidence that this achievement gap narrows when socio-economic 
factors are taken into account.
The relationship between home life and schooling outcomes can be examined in TIMSS. Children in homes 
with many resources for learning had higher mathematics achievement, on average, than those whose homes 
had fewer resources. Students who often did early literacy and numeracy learning activities with their parents 
(according to the parents) had higher mathematics achievement, on average, than students who did so less 
often. Students who had attended an early childhood facility for more than 1 year had higher mathematics 
achievement by Year 5 than those who had attended for less time, or not at all.
It was clear from the TIMSS results that some schools face more barriers to helping their Year 5 students 
achieve than others. Mathematics achievement was higher, on average, for students in schools with more 
affluent students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The difference in 
mathematics achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was higher than most other 
countries in the TIMSS study.
Parents reports show that many students begin school already having some numeracy abilities, and that this 
early preparation has a positive effect on their achievement later on. According to principals’ estimates of 
the numeracy abilities of students when they began school, mathematics achievement at Year 5 was higher 
in schools where the cohort were more mathematically able when they were new entrants. In general, more 
affluent schools were more likely to report higher proportions of able students in their school intake.
Resources available at school could help offset the lack of resources at home. TIMSS asked about the level to 
which lack of resources at school were a problem. Teachers most commonly reported lack of technological 
resources, lack of support for using technology, and inadequate workspace for teachers as moderate or 
serious problems at their school. New Zealand Year 5 teachers reported fewer problems, in general, than many 
of their international counterparts. Interestingly, at the Year 5 level, New Zealand had the highest proportion of 
students with computers available for use during mathematics lessons, compared to all other countries.
Principals most commonly reported a lack of technologically competent staff hindered instruction at their 
school. Other resources commonly reported as hindering instruction included a lack of teachers with a 
specialisation in mathematics, computer software, library resources for mathematics instruction, computers 
for teaching and learning, and resources for students with disabilities. The less affected the principal 
felt instruction at the school was by a shortage of mathematics resources, the higher the mathematics 
achievement scores.
Student and parental attitudes to learning mathematics
To what extent do parent and student attitudes relate to mathematics achievement? Parental attitudes 
towards mathematics and science did not seem to have a direct effect on students’ mathematics 
achievement in the TIMSS assessment.
The average (mean) mathematics achievement score was higher for the group of students who read every 
day or almost every day and declined as the amount of time spent on reading reduced. New Zealand students 
spent more time on sports as an out of school activity compared to other activities which were culturally 
focussed. Tutoring in mathematics was less common in New Zealand than among high-performing countries.
More than three quarters of New Zealand Year 5 students liked learning mathematics. However compared 
to the international average, New Zealand Year 5 students were a bit more negative about mathematics. 
While the majority of New Zealand Year 5 students were confident in mathematics, almost one third were 
not confident. New Zealand had more not confident and less very confident students in mathematics, 

162
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents
compared with international averages. Students had higher mathematics achievement, on average, if they 
were confident in their ability or liked learning mathematics. The mathematics achievement of students had a 
stronger relationship with their confidence in mathematics than how much they liked learning.
More New Zealand Year 5 boys were very confident in mathematics, while more girls were not confident. 
This also held across all ethnic groupings, except for Māori. Māori Year 5 girls and boys had very similar 
proportions both for liking and confidence. The Pasifika grouping stood out with a bigger proportion (more 
than half) of students very much liking to learn mathematics, while the Pākehā/European grouping had 
almost thirty percent of students not liking to learn mathematics. 
Teachers and teaching mathematics
Blömeke, Olsen, & Suhl (2016), in their recent research paper using TIMSS 2010/11 results, found that teacher 
qualifications and measures of instructional quality were both significantly related to student outcomes. 
More New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers with postgraduate and higher degrees in 2014/15 compared 
to 2010/11 (27% compared to 19%). About one-fifth of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who 
had also specialised in mathematics. Internationally, more students (43% on average) had teachers with a 
specialisation in mathematics. Compared to most of the participating countries, more New Zealand teachers 
participated in mathematics related professional development activities. 
New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about how their teacher engaged with them in their mathematics 
classes, but not as positive as on average internationally. A very high proportion of students in New Zealand 
had teachers report that in every or almost every lesson, they encouraged their students to express their 
ideas and encouraged classroom discussion among students.
New Zealand teachers made less use of whole class teaching compared to other countries, and used group 
activities more often. New Zealand teachers had high use of same ability grouping and relatively high use of 
having students work on problems (individually or with peers) while the teacher was occupied by other tasks. 
Activities where teachers explained new mathematics content, teachers explained how to solve problems, 
or teachers asked students to memorise rules, facts and procedures were less likely to be used in every or 
almost every lesson in New Zealand classrooms, compared with other countries. 
More Year 5 students had mathematics teachers who placed major emphasis on the evaluation of students’ 
ongoing work than on classroom or nationally available tests. On average internationally, many more teachers 
placed major emphasis on classroom tests than in New Zealand.
School climate for learning
A positive school climate has been shown to be associated with academic achievement, as well as 
behavioural and social-emotional wellbeing of students (see for example Voight, Austin, & Hanson, 2013). 
The TIMSS results for New Zealand confirm this finding when the climate is examined from the point of view 
of students, teachers, principals and parents. 
On average, nine out of ten New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about their schools, teachers 
and classmates, and two-thirds had a high sense of school belonging. Students who had a high sense of 
belonging had higher mathematics achievement.
There were more students in this latest cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that they felt safe at school, 
liked being in school, and felt like they belonged at their school. However, New Zealand Year 5 students 
experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than most of the other participating countries. 
Compared to 2010/11, fewer students experienced the individual bullying behaviours: I was made fun of or 
called names, something was stolen from me, and I was hit or hurt (e.g shoving, hitting, kicking) by other 
student(s). For all other behaviours, the proportion did not change significantly between the two cycles. 
More boys experienced bullying behaviours than girls. More students experienced bullying behaviours in 
schools with lower socio-economic status. Those experiencing bullying behaviours more often had lower in 
mathematics achievement, on average. 
So what did the teachers think about the climate for learning? The majority of New Zealand schools (80% 
of students) had high or very high emphasis on academic success, according to teachers. Higher school 
emphasis on academic success was associated with higher mathematics scores, both in New Zealand and 
internationally.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
163
TIMSS 2014/15 | MATHEMATICS | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»
Half of the Year 5 students were taught by teachers who were very satisfied with their job; 96 percent of 
teachers were proud of the work they did. Seventy-one percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers 
who felt their school was very safe and orderly.
Compared with many other countries, New Zealand teachers perceived more challenges in their teaching. 
They wanted to have more time to assist individual students, and preferred to have less administrative tasks. 
Three quarters of students studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs were somewhat limiting 
their mathematics teaching. These students had lower achievement than those who studied in classes where 
teachers felt that student needs did not limit teaching at all.
The principals were very positive about their teachers, with more than 80 percent of students having 
principals who rated teachers’ role in the school’s academic success as very high or high. Principals were less 
positive about parents’ support and involvement than about their teachers and students. The principals and 
teachers had similar views on most aspects of the schools’ academic success, the only difference of opinion 
was observed on the students’ respect for classmates who excel in school, about which the principals were 
more positive than the teachers.
Compared to other countries, New Zealand principals reported fewer problems of school discipline and 
safety. Over two-thirds of Year 5 students in New Zealand (71%) attended schools where the principal 
reported hardly any problems with school discipline and safety. Being in a school with less of a problem 
was associated with higher achievement in mathematics, on average. There has been no change in the 
level of discipline and safety in New Zealand schools since TIMSS 2010/11. This finding appears to contrast 
with student reports of bullying behaviours; New Zealand was one of the countries with the most students 
regularly experiencing bullying behaviours.
In general, parents had a very positive perception of their child’s school. Students whose parents were less 
than satisfied with their child’s school had lower mathematics achievement than those who were more 
satisfied. Nearly all parents (97%) agreed that their child’s school provided a safe environment for learning. 
Students in these schools had a lot higher achievement than those whose parents thought their school didn’t 
provide a safe environment. A higher percentage of parents felt that schools were doing a good job in helping 
the students to become better in mathematics or reading than in science. Compared to 2010/11, in 2014/15 
more parents agreed that their child’s school does a good job in helping him/her become better in science.
Final comment
As well as providing us with a snapshot of student achievement in mathematics in middle primary and 
lower secondary schooling, TIMSS also provides us with valuable information about how the New Zealand 
education system changes — or does not — over time and in an international context. This allows education 
stakeholders at all levels of the education system to reflect on the different aspects examined in TIMSS, as 
part of a review of their policies and practices.

quality and student outcomes: Relationships across countries, cohorts and time. Switzerland: Springer

Sampling
Schools were sampled for Year 5 and Year 9 
separately but with the intent to minimise the 
overlap and therefore to minimise the burden 
on individual schools. They were sampled from 
pre-defined groups. These pre-defined groups, 
or explicit strata, were based on decile, level of 
urbanisation, and for Year 9 only, school gender. 
This methodology meant that the schools selected 
better represented the population of schools in 
New Zealand. Within each school, at the Year 5 
level, classes were sampled with equal probability, 
and all Year 5 students within each sampled class 
were selected. At the Year 9 level, 2 classes were 
sampled and all Year 9 students within each 
sampled class were selected.
Exclusions
Each country was permitted to exclude some 
students for whom the assessment was not 
appropriate or was difficult to administer. Countries 
were required to keep the amount of excluded 
students as small as possible, with a guideline 
of five percent of the ’target’ population as the 
maximum. Any countries that exceeded this 
value are indicated in the international exhibits. 
The target population in New Zealand for middle 
primary students was Year 5 students and for lower 
secondary was Year 9 students.
Definitions and 
technical notes
This section gives a brief overview of the 
technical details and definitions applicable 
to this report. For a comprehensive 
description of the technical details 
pertaining to TIMSS see Methods and 
Procedures in TIMSS 2015 (Martin, Mullis, 
& Hooper, (Eds.), 2016).
School-level exclusions in New Zealand consisted of 
very small schools (less than four Year 5 students 
or less than four Year 9 students), special education 
schools, the Correspondence School, and schools 
that provide more than 50% of their instruction in 
te reo Māori. Within-school exclusions consisted of 
special education classes, special needs students, 
students with insufficient instruction in English, and 
units within schools that provide more than 50% of 
their instruction in te reo Māori.
The New Zealand exclusion rate was 4.8% for Year 
5 and 3.1% for Year 9. 
Mean, medians, modes and 
averages
There are three measures of central tendancy, 
the mean, median, and mode. The mean of a set 
of scores is the sum of the scores divided by the 
number of scores, and is also sometimes referred 
to as ’the average’, particularly in the international 
reports. Note that for TIMSS, as with other large-
scale studies, the means for a country are adjusted 
slightly (in technical terms ’weighted’) to reflect the 
total population of Year 5 students, rather than just 
the sample. The mode is the value that appears the 
most out of all values. The median is the middle 
value when all the values are put in order.

Scale score points
The design of TIMSS allows for a large number 
of questions to be used in mathematics and 
science; each student answers only a portion of 
these questions. TIMSS employs techniques to 
enable population estimates of achievement to be 
produced for each country, even though a sample 
of students responded to differing selections of 
questions. These techniques result in scaled scores 
that are on a scale with a mean of 500 and a 
standard deviation of 100.
TIMSS scale centrepoint
In order to make comparisons, student 
achievement scores generated in each cycle 
are placed on the same scale. The scale was 
established during the second cycle of TIMSS to 
have a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 
100, based on the mean of country means from 
1994/95. Equating is possible because a proportion 
of questions are the same in each assessment 
as for the two previous cycles. A score of 500 
in 2014/15 is the same as a score of 500 in all 
previous cycles.
In earlier cycles of TIMSS, an international mean 
was reported. However as the number of countries 
participating changed, this mean shifted so that 
it was difficult to make comparisons across years. 
Starting from TIMSS 2010/11, only the TIMSS scale 
centrepoint of 500 is reported. This is the same 
as the TIMSS scale average reported in previous 
cycles, but renamed to avoid confusion with a 
calculated mean of country means. 
Minimum group size for 
reporting achievement data
In this report, student achievement data is not 
reported where the group size is less than 50 
students or less than 10 schools. 
Percentile
The percentages of students performing below or 
above particular points on the scale can be used 
to describe the range of achievement. The lowest 
outer limit of achievement reported in ranges is 
the 5th percentile – the score at which only five 
percent of students achieved a lower score, and 
95% of students achieved a higher score. The 
highest outer limit is the 95th percentile – the score 
at which only five percent of students achieved a 
higher score, and 95% of students achieved a lower 
score. Therefore 90% of the Year 5 student scores 
lie between the 5th and 95th percentiles.
The range of achievement
In this report, the range of achievement is the 
difference between the low percentile score 
(the score at which only five percent of students 
achieved a lower score) and the high percentile 
score (the score at which only five percent of 
students achieved a higher score).
Inter-quartile range
The inter-quartile range is more stable than the 
full range and is the difference between the 
25th percentile score (the score at which 25% 
of students achieved a lower score) and 75th 
percentile score (the score at which 25% of 
students achieved a higher score).
Benchmarks
In order to describe more fully what achievement 
on the mathematics scale means, the TIMSS 
international researchers have developed 
benchmarks. These benchmarks link student 
performance on the TIMSS mathematics scale 
to performance on mathematics questions and 
describe what students can typically do at set 
points on the mathematics achievement scale. 
The international mathematics benchmarks are four 
points on the mathematics scale; the advanced 
benchmark (625), the high benchmark (550), 
the intermediate benchmark (475), and the low 
benchmark (400). The performance of students 
reaching each benchmark is described in relation to 
the types of questions they answered correctly.
Content domains
These are the content areas of mathematics, known 
as domains, covered by the TIMSS assessment 
framework. The content of mathematics at 
the middle primary level is made up of three 
dimensions or domains; number, geometric shapes 
and measures, and data display. (see chapter 3 for 
details).
Cognitive domains
The cognitive domains are the thinking processes 
and behaviours expected as students engage with 
the content ranging from simple knowledge recall 
to higher-order thinking skills, and include knowing, 
applying, and reasoning dimensions (see chapter 3 
for details).

Test-curriculum matching 
analysis
In order to investigate the extent to which the 
TIMSS assessment is relevant to each country’s 
curriculum, TIMSS conducts a Test-Curriculum 
Matching Analysis (TCMA). The TCMA is also 
used to investigate the impact of selecting only 
appropriate questions on a country’s performance. 
(for details see the TIMSS International 
Database guide) 
Created scales for 
contextual variables
Item response theory was applied to student 
responses to questions so that clustering was 
taken into account. For example if nearly every 
student gave a highly positive response to one item 
then it did not overly contribute to the sum. Each 
respondent was then given a score which was put 
on a scale. Cut points on that scale were defined 
and descriptions provided that detailed the kind of 
responses given in the original questions.
Ethnicity classification in 
TIMSS 2014/15
In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand 
classified each student to one ethnic grouping 
using only prioritisation. In this cycle, any student 
who identified with more than one grouping was 
assigned to each of those groupings, in line with 
the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classification 
(Statistics New Zealand, 2005). TIMSS 2014/15 
classified the following ethnic groupings based on 
student responses: Māori, Pasifika, Asian, Pākehā/
European, and Other. If a student ticked both Māori 
and Samoan, they would be classified as both 
Māori and Pasifika. Using this methodology, ethnic 
groupings cannot be compared with each other 
as 22% of students are in more than one grouping 
(some are in as many as four groupings).
PIRLS
Stands for the Progress in International Reading 
Literacy Study and is being conducted under the 
auspices of the IEA (International Association 
for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement); 
managed internationally by Boston College; and 
managed within New Zealand by the Ministry of 
Education. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pirls
PISA 
Programme for International Student Assessment 
(PISA) is a triennial international survey which 
aims to evaluate education systems worldwide 
by testing the skills and knowledge of 15-year-old 
students. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa
School decile
School deciles indicate the extent the school 
draws their students from low socio-economic 
communities. It is calculated every 5 years following 
the Census of Population and Dwellings, and based 
on 5 socio-economic indicators for a community: 
»»
Percentage of households with income in the 
lowest 20% nationally.
»»
Percentage of employed parents in the lowest 
skill level occupational groups.
»»
Household crowding.
»»
Percentage of parents with no educational 
qualifications.
»»
Percentage of parents receiving income 
support benefits.
For more information visit: http://www.education.
govt.nz/school/running-a-school/resourcing/
operational-funding/school-decile-ratings/#About

the t statistic, with the probability of making an 
incorrect inference set at five percent. To compare 
the means of two groups of students, the formula 
to generate the test statistics computed in this 
report is:

where the sei are the standard errors of all the

Standard error
Because of the technical nature of TIMSS, the 
calculation of statistics such as means and 
proportions has some uncertainty due to (i) 
generalising from the sample to the total Year 5 
school population, and (ii) inferring each student’s 
proficiency from their performance on a subset 
of questions. The standard errors provide a 
measure of this uncertainty. In general, we can be 
95% confident that the true population value lies 
within an interval of 1.96 standard errors either 
side of the given statistic. This confidence interval 
is represented in graphs by the lines extending in 
either direction from the points.
In this cycle of TIMSS, a new method for calculating 
the standard errors has been used. Therefore, the 
standard errors for previous cycles have been 
recalculated using this new method and will be 
different from those reported earlier. This new 
methodology takes advantage of advances in 
computing and uses more information about 
variations in computations.
Statistically significant
In order to determine whether a difference 
between two means is actual, it is usual to 
undertake tests of significance. These tests take 
into account the means and the error associated 
with them. If a result is reported as not being 
statistically significant then, although the means 
might be slightly different, we do not have 
sufficient evidence to infer that they are different. 
All tests of statistical significance referred to in this 
report are at the 95% confidence level.
Weighting
Due to the use of sampling, weights need to be 
applied when analysing the TIMSS data. Weighting 
ensures that any information presented more 
closely reflects the total population of Year 5 
students, rather than just the sample. The TIMSS 
weighting takes into account school, class, and 
student level information, and the overall sampling 
weight is a product of the school, class, and 
student weights.
